== #systems-and-networking: 310 lines before, 313 after; as printed only before 24, only after 27; marks out only before 1, only after 4; card 49 lines, the same words; order with the card set aside the same
   sources: 88 lines before, 88 after, the same set, in the new reading order
   the card before: after "An eight-GPU baseboard, the board that carries eight accelerator modul", before "In plain terms"
   the card after:  after "Before a chip package can compute, it has to be built into a machine. ", before "How it works#"
   words only before: High
   words only after:  High concentration
   words only after:  ·
   words only after:  Moderately hard to substitute
   words only after:  Chokepoint card
   - High
   - Rated on NVLink and its NVSwitch chips, which Nvidia controls and which join the accelerators in a rack1. Separately, lasers for the optical links were scarce in 20262. Nvidia secured future capacity rights for them from Lumentum in March 20263.
   - for one GB200 NVL72 rack bought by a typical hyperscaler, or about $3.9 million with networking, storage and other items, by SemiAnalysis's August 2025 estimate4
   - NVLink, InfiniBand and Spectrum-X Ethernet, which it credited for data center growth in the quarter to April 20265
   - $16.7B of AI semiconductor revenue in the quarter to August 2026, up 221%6
   - World's largest AI server provider, its chairman said in March 20267
   - High-speed optical modules made in Suzhou and, since 2019, Thailand8
   - Lasers and optical networking products, with a $2B Nvidia investment agreed in March 20269
   - UALink is an open specification for the same job as NVLink10. AMD's Helios rack, launched in July 2026, runs it over Ethernet11. Since May 2025 Nvidia's NVLink Fusion program has let other firms' custom chips use NVLink, which keeps those firms tied to Nvidia's interconnect12.
   - Chinese firms such as InnoLight assemble high-speed optical modules in volume, at sites including Suzhou and, since 2019, Thailand8. In February 2025 China put indium under new export restrictions13. The same month it began requiring export permits for indium phosphide substrates, the wafers that lasers for optical links are grown on14.
   - In March 2026 Nvidia agreed to invest $2 billion in the laser maker Lumentum, alongside a multibillion-dollar purchase commitment and rights to future capacity for advanced laser components3. Indium phosphide is used in high-speed lasers for optical communications, and China produced 70 percent of the world's indium in 202513.
   - On 4 February 2025 China added indium phosphide substrates, the wafers the lasers are grown on, to its export control list14. AXT, a US-listed maker of those wafers, does all its manufacturing in China14. In its filing for the quarter to June 2026, AXT said it could not estimate when it would resume shipping the wafers to the United States14.
   - Rack-scale systems drop the baseboard and put the chips on trays stacked in the rack. An Nvidia GB300 NVL72 holds 18 compute trays plus nine switch trays, 72 GPUs in all, and draws up to 142 kW17. The newer Vera Rubin NVL72 uses the same rack footprint18. The research firm SemiAnalysis estimated in August 2025 that a rack of the earlier GB200 NVL72 model cost a typical hyperscaler $3.1 million, or about $3.9 million with networking, storage and other items4.
   - UALink. The open specification, from the UALink Consortium, runs 200 Gb/s per lane, and four lanes, which it calls a station, carry 800 Gb/s10. It can address up to 1,024 endpoints, such as accelerators, in one group10. AMD's Helios rack, launched in July 2026, joins 72 GPUs and runs UALink over Ethernet for its scale-up network11.
   - Nvidia sells switches and network cards for both InfiniBand and Ethernet. Its data center revenue, which counts its GPUs as well as its networking gear, reached $89.0 billion in the quarter to July 2026, up 117 percent from a year earlier29. In the quarter before, it credited its data center growth to Blackwell 300 products and to demand for its "InfiniBand, Spectrum-X Ethernet, and NVLink" solutions5.
   - Chinese makers of optical modules have built plants outside China. InnoLight has run a plant in Thailand since 2019, alongside its sites in Suzhou and Taiwan8. The Thai subsidiary of Eoptolink, another Chinese module maker, lists sites in Chonburi and Rayong33.
   - China produces most of the world's indium, the raw material for indium phosphide lasers, and restricts its export. Indium phosphide is used in the high-speed laser diodes and photodetectors for optical communications13. China accounted for 70 percent of world indium production in 202513. In February 2025 China's Ministry of Commerce placed indium under new export restrictions13.
   - AI data centers were straining supplies of the lasers inside optical modules and pushing up their prices, Nikkei Asia reported in May 20262. Nvidia had agreed in March 2026 to invest $2 billion in one laser maker, Lumentum, alongside a multibillion-dollar purchase commitment and rights to future capacity for advanced laser components3. Coherent, another laser maker, broke ground in Sherman, Texas, in June 2026 on an expansion of its factory there, which makes optical devices from indium phosphide15.
   - Two American firms, Broadcom and Marvell, sell both custom AI chips and Ethernet switch chips. In its annual report for fiscal 2025, Broadcom said its AI chips include Ethernet switch chips as well as custom accelerators, which it builds to each customer's specifications39. Its AI semiconductor revenue reached $16.7 billion in the third quarter of fiscal 2026, up 221 percent from a year earlier6. Marvell also builds custom AI chips to each customer's specifications and sells Ethernet switch chips, its annual report for fiscal 2026 says40. Marvell's five-year agreement with Amazon Web Services, announced in December 2024, covers custom AI chips alongside the optical and networking parts that go with them41.
   - Taiwanese firms do much of the physical assembly. Foxconn booked NT$8.1 trillion of revenue in 2025, up 18 percent, and cloud and networking was its largest product category in the fourth quarter7. In the same results release, its chairman called Foxconn the world's largest AI server provider7.
   - Indium phosphide laser capacity. Nvidia has secured rights to future capacity from laser makers. Alongside its Lumentum deal, in March 2026 Nvidia agreed to invest $2 billion in Coherent9. The deal also includes a multibillion-dollar purchase commitment and rights to future access and capacity for Coherent's advanced laser and optical networking products9.
   - Indium phosphide substrates. China added indium phosphide substrates, the wafers the lasers are grown on, to its export control list on 4 February 202514. AXT, a US-listed maker of these wafers, does all its manufacturing in China14. In its filing for the quarter to June 2026, AXT called the need for Chinese export permits the most significant challenge it faces14. It said it could not estimate when it would resume shipping the wafers to the United States14. The same filing names Sumitomo and JX, both Japanese firms, as AXT's competitors in indium phosphide14.
   - NVSwitch. Nvidia's annual report for fiscal 2026 lists NVLink interconnects and switches among its own networking products1. UALink is an open standard designed for accelerator communications within one scale-up group10. Since May 2025 Nvidia's NVLink Fusion program has let other firms' custom chips use NVLink, including CPUs that Fujitsu and Qualcomm plan to connect to Nvidia GPUs12.
   - Optical module assembly can move, as InnoLight's high-volume plant in Thailand, opened in 2019, shows8.

It said it could not estimate when it would resume shipping the wafers to the United States3. The same filing names Sumitomo and JX, both Japanese firms, as AXT's competitors in indium phosphide3.
   + NVSwitch. Nvidia's annual report for fiscal 2026 lists NVLink interconnects and switches among its own networking products4. UALink is an open standard designed for accelerator communications within one scale-up group12. Since May 2025 Nvidia's NVLink Fusion program has let other firms' custom chips use NVLink, including CPUs that Fujitsu and Qualcomm plan to connect to Nvidia GPUs14.
   + Optical module assembly can move, as InnoLight's high-volume plant in Thailand, opened in 2019, shows10.
== #data-centers-and-power: 331 lines before, 334 after; as printed only before 39, only after 42; marks out only before 1, only after 4; card 49 lines, the same words; order with the card set aside the same
   sources: 72 lines before, 72 after, the same set, in the new reading order

   the card after:  after "A wafer passes through a leading-edge fab in months, but in 2024 a lar", before "How it works#"

   - High
   - Nvidia named nine data center power system providers in October 2025 for its 800-volt designs for gigawatt-scale data centers1.
   - of data center investment expected worldwide in 2025, in the International Energy Agency's November 2025 outlook2
   - Gas equipment, transformers and switchgear, including through Prolec GE3
   - Power transformers and switchgear3
   - Grid technologies, transformers, turbines3
   - Power generation equipment, including gas turbines3
   - Data center power systems1
   - Cooling, through its Motivair division4
   - Buyers of grid equipment can choose among suppliers, and GE Vernova's 2025 annual report names Hitachi Energy, Siemens Energy, Schneider Electric and ABB among its key competitors3. But in the United States in 2024 the longest large power transformer lead times reached 60 months5. An International Energy Agency survey published in 2025 found waits of up to four years worldwide6.
   - China used 25 percent of the world's data center electricity in 2024, second to the United States, by the International Energy Agency's estimate7. Data centers made up around 3 percent of the growth in China's electricity demand from 2022 to 20248.
   - US factories supplied 29.2 percent of the large power transformers bought in the United States in 2023, measured by capacity9. Constellation said in August 2026 that it expects to restart the Crane nuclear plant in 202710. Microsoft has agreed to buy the plant's power11.
   - A data center feeds electricity to the machines inside it and carries away the heat they give off. One rack of AI chips, a cabinet taller than a door, can draw 142 kilowatts12. Every watt of it comes back out as heat, and liquid piped through the rack carries the heat away12. A large training site already holds more than a hundred thousand chips13. The newest sites are planned in gigawatts14. One gigawatt is the whole output of a large power station, and Epoch AI projects that OpenAI's seven Stargate sites in the United States will add up to more than nine of them by 202914. Getting that much electricity to the site is harder than putting up the building. Transformers and heavy switches connect a site to the grid, and turbines make the power. All of them come from a few suppliers and take years to arrive.
   - Measured by capacity, US factories supplied 29.2 percent of the large power transformers bought in the US in 2023, and imports the rest9. GE Vernova's gas equipment backlog and reserved factory slots stood at 116 GW in mid-2026, against planned output of 20 GW a year from the third quarter15.
   - ERCOT, the grid operator for most of Texas, counted 9,456 MW of big power users approved to energize in its overview for September 202616. Judging by peak monthly use in September, it believed 4,132 MW of that was operating16. On 3 August 2026 Governor Greg Abbott ordered an audit of every data center seeking a connection before any data center project moves forward17. In September 2026 ERCOT said it had paused approvals to energize data centers of 75 MW or more until the audit and a review of their impact on local communities are complete18.
   - A wafer passes through a leading-edge fab in months, but in 2024 a large power transformer commonly took three years to arrive5. Before the pandemic, the same order took under a year19.
   - The rack is the basic unit of AI compute. Nvidia's GB300 NVL72 rack draws up to 142 kW through eight 33 kW power shelves, the rack's own power supplies12. Large sites hold many such racks. Colossus 1, an AI data center in Memphis, Tennessee, held an estimated 230,000 Nvidia GPUs by July 202513.
   - Heat leaves in water. Cold plates, metal blocks with coolant running through them, sit on the GPU packages and pass the heat to the building's water loop, which releases it outdoors4. Nvidia's Rubin racks run coolant at up to 45 °C, warm enough that in many climates the loop can release its heat without turning on chillers, the refrigeration machines that cool water4.
   - Air. Heat exchangers in the rack door work when density, the power each rack draws, is moderate, but Nvidia's GB300 NVL72 rack is liquid cooled12.
   - Direct-to-chip liquid. Nvidia's Rubin platform is 100 percent liquid cooled, and Nvidia says every cloud provider and data center operator building for it is moving to liquid4.
   - Nvidia says the cooling supply chain is keeping pace with the move to liquid4. Motivair, Schneider Electric's cooling division, has worked with Nvidia for nearly a decade, following Nvidia's product roadmap4.
   - Nvidia names nine data center power system providers for its 800-volt designs for gigawatt-scale data centers, among them ABB, Eaton, Hitachi Energy, Mitsubishi Electric, Schneider Electric, Siemens and Vertiv1.
   - A megawatt rack changes the busbar, the floor loading, the coolant flow, the leak detection and the energy stored in the rack. Nvidia's Vera Rubin NVL72 rack design adds energy storage to keep power steady1.
   - Grid interconnection. The slow path in the United States, where the queues to connect are long14.
   - Behind-the-meter gas, generation on the site itself. The fast path, which at least three of OpenAI's seven US Stargate sites will use to avoid grid queues14.
   - Nuclear. Existing plants already sell power to data centers22. Talen Energy supplies Amazon's data center campus next to the Susquehanna nuclear plant in Pennsylvania under a June 2025 contract expected to reach 1,920 MW no later than 203222. Restarts and new reactors are still to come. Constellation signed a power purchase agreement with Microsoft to restart Three Mile Island Unit 1 as the 835 MW Crane Clean Energy Center11. The plant shut in 201923. A $1 billion federal loan that closed in November 2025 partly finances its restart, which is subject to licensing approvals from the US Nuclear Regulatory Commission23.
   - The largest data center operators pay for the buildout. Capital spending by Alphabet, Amazon, Meta, Microsoft and Oracle came to $448.2 billion in 2025, the sum of quarterly figures compiled by Epoch AI, a research group24. If the trend continues, it will reach $770 billion in 202624. The International Energy Agency expected worldwide investment in data centers to reach $580 billion in 20252.
   - Epoch AI also projects that OpenAI's seven US Stargate sites will exceed 9 GW by 202914. It expects the site in Abilene, Texas, to grow from an estimated 0.3 GW in April 2026 to 1.2 GW in the fourth quarter of 202614.
   - A few large firms make the turbines, transformers and switchgear. GE Vernova builds large gas turbines, and its 2025 annual report names Siemens Energy and Mitsubishi Power among the key competitors of its power generation business3. Its electrification business makes power transformers and switchgear, and the report names Hitachi Energy, Siemens Energy, Schneider Electric and ABB among that business's key competitors3.
   - Equipment makers limit how fast the buildout can go. GE Vernova's gas equipment backlog and reserved factory slots grew from 100 GW to 116 GW in the second quarter of 202615. In the first half of 2026, data center orders for its electrification business, which makes grid equipment, passed $5 billion, more than double that business's data center orders for all of 202515. GE Vernova bought the remaining half of Prolec GE, a transformer maker, in the first half of 202615.
   - Source GE Vernova second-quarter 2026 results, July 2026 15
   - The US Energy Information Administration expects electricity demand to keep rising, but in September 2026 it cut its 2027 forecast for the region that includes Texas. In its October 2026 Short-Term Energy Outlook, it expects US electricity sales of 4,152.0 TWh in 2026 and 4,216.6 TWh in 202727. It expects electricity demand from the commercial sector, which includes data centers, to grow 2.8 percent in 2027 and demand from industry to grow 2.7 percent28. Its September outlook had lowered the 2027 electricity sales forecast for the region that includes Texas by 4.7 percent from August29. That outlook noted that Texas had paused connecting new data centers to the grid while the state audits proposed projects30. The pause, set by the grid operator for most of Texas, covers data centers and crypto mining sites of 75 MW or more18.
   - By the International Energy Agency's estimate, China accounted for 25 percent of the world's data center electricity use in 2024, second to the United States at 45 percent7. Data centers made up around 3 percent of the growth in China's electricity demand from 2022 to 2024, the agency found8.
   - On the Texas grid, big power users have asked for far more electricity than is connected and running. ERCOT, the grid operator for most of Texas, was tracking about 474 GW of large loads (big power users) seeking a connection in June 2026, roughly 90 percent of it data centers32. In its operational overview for September 2026, ERCOT counted 9,456 MW of large loads approved to energize, that is, cleared to switch on16. Judging by peak monthly use in September, it believed only 4,132 MW of it was operating16.
   - Before a big user can connect, engineers must study whether the nearby grid can carry its load. In a preliminary review in July 2026, ERCOT found that about 205 GW of the requests already had such a study, which made them eligible for Batch Zero, the first batch of requests it studies together33. In September 2026 ERCOT conditionally classed 66.4 GW of Batch Zero projects as base load and 127.9 GW as studied load, pending final decisions in December18. Under ERCOT's Batch Zero rules, base load is demand from projects already running or far enough along to be counted without further study34. Studied load is demand that ERCOT will study before giving each project a share of the grid's capacity34.
   - Texas has paused new connections for large data centers while it audits them. On 3 August 2026 Texas Governor Greg Abbott ordered the state's Public Utility Commission and ERCOT to audit every data center waiting to connect to the grid before any data center project moves forward17. ERCOT then paused approvals to energize data centers and crypto mining sites of 75 MW or more until the audit and a review of their impact on local communities are complete18. It will report on the audit and the community review to the Public Utility Commission by 10 December 202618.
   - Large power transformers. In the United States in 2024, lead times of 36 months were commonly quoted and the longest reached 60 months, against under a year before the pandemic5. By a US Commerce Department estimate, 82 percent of the units put into US service in 2019 were imported35. In an industry survey published in 2025, the International Energy Agency found that large power transformers took up to four years to secure worldwide6. It also found that their average lead times had almost doubled since 20216. A transformer's size is given as a power rating in megavolt-amperes (MVA)5. Measured by capacity, US factories supplied 29.2 percent of the transformers rated 60 MVA and above bought in the US in 2023, and imports supplied the rest9.
   - Gas turbines. GE Vernova's gas equipment backlog and reserved factory slots stood at 116 GW in mid-202615. In July 2026 it expected to build gas turbines at a yearly rate of 20 GW in the third quarter of 2026, 24 GW in 2028 and 30 GW in 203015.
   - Firm generation, the kind that runs whatever the weather. The Nuclear Regulatory Commission has approved a fuel license amendment for the Crane plant10. Constellation called the approval a major milestone toward restarting the plant in 202710.

== /design-and-eda/: 316 lines before, 319 after; as printed only before 35, only after 38; marks out only before 1, only after 4; card 49 lines, the same words; order with the card set aside the same
   sources: 60 lines before, 60 after, the same set, in the new reading order
   the card before: after "See it in 3D in the interactive chapter", before "In plain terms"
   the card after:  after "Design is the only stage of the supply chain that happens entirely in ", before "How it works"
   words only before: Extreme
   words only after:  Extreme concentration
   words only after:  ·
   words only after:  Hard to substitute
   words only after:  Chokepoint card
   - Extreme
   - Only three complete sets of chip design software exist[1]. A fourth would have to be written from nothing and approved by a foundry[2].
   - world market for design software, simulation software and licensed circuit blocks in 2025[2]. SemiAnalysis put a single respin, a design revision that needs some new masks, at leading-edge nodes at $50-100M in May 2026[2]
   - 90%+ of the timing-signoff market at advanced nodes as of 2026[2]
   - 55-60% of the hardware emulator market at advanced nodes as of 2026[2]
   - 85%+ of physical verification, the final layout check, at advanced nodes as of 2026[2]
   - About 50% of processor compute at the top cloud firms as of May 2026[3]
   - $16.7B of AI semiconductor revenue in the third quarter of fiscal 2026, custom accelerators and networking combined[4]
   - Changing one tool, such as the synthesis tool that turns a design's code into logic cells, means re-running the later steps of the design[2]. The foundry that makes the chip also specifies which signoff tools its customers must use[2].
   - China designs its own AI accelerators, but Huawei's most powerful chip in 2026, the Ascend 950, delivers about half the computing performance of Nvidia's H100 from 2022, on Epoch AI's estimate[5]. CSIS found that as of 2025 Chinese design tools were still far from replacing the full range of advanced capabilities US firms offer, especially for 5 nm and 3 nm chips[6].
   - Synopsys and Cadence, the two largest design-software vendors by 2025 revenue, are American[2].
   - Chip design turns an idea for a new chip into a complete blueprint. A chip holds billions of transistors, tiny electrical switches. The blueprint fixes where each one sits and how it is wired to the others. The chip runs on a clock, and a signal has to cross those wires within one tick, less than a billionth of a second. Software from three companies, Synopsys, Cadence and Siemens, lays out the blueprint, checks it and signs it off. A foundry, a factory that makes chips for other companies, accepts only designs that have passed that software's checks. The foundry prints each layer of the design onto silicon through a stencil called a mask. Changing the blueprint once the masks are made takes new masks and a few months[7].
   - The US government required a license to sell design software to anyone in China from late May to 2 July 2025[8]. Since October 2022 a narrower requirement has covered software specially made for chips with gate-all-around transistors, a transistor design the rule calls key to processes at 3 nm and below[9].
   - As of 2025, the think tank CSIS found Chinese design tools still far from replacing the full range of advanced capabilities US firms offer, especially for 5 nm and 3 nm chip design[6]. But Chinese design-software companies were winning share at older manufacturing processes, the research firm SemiAnalysis reported[2].
   - Design is the only stage of the supply chain that happens entirely in software. The US government has restricted it with letters to the companies that sell that software. In late May 2025 the government required a license to sell chip design software to anyone in China[8]. Cadence cut off its Chinese customers until the government lifted the requirement on 2 July[8].
   - All that checking exists because each revision after tape-out, called a respin, needs at least some new masks[7]. In 2022 SemiAnalysis put a full mask set at more than $1 million at 28 nm and more than $10 million at 7 nm[7]. The nanometer labels name process generations, called nodes, each finer than the last, and no longer measure anything. In 2022 SemiAnalysis also expected 3 nm sets to push into the $40 million range[7]. Its May 2026 primer still puts a leading-edge mask set at $40 million, and a single leading-edge respin at $50 million to $100 million[2].
   - The same 2022 SemiAnalysis article reported that startups had taken chips on TSMC 7 nm, then a leading-edge process, from design to tape-out for $50 million to $75 million, everything included[7].
   - Source SemiAnalysis, The Dark Side of the Semiconductor Design Renaissance, July 2022. The 3 nm figure was a 2022 forecast. SemiAnalysis's May 2026 EDA Market Primer puts a leading-edge mask set at $40M[7]
   - Chip design software is known as electronic design automation, or EDA. Three complete EDA flows exist, each a set of tools that covers every step from code to signoff[1]. SemiAnalysis wrote in May 2026 that no startup can replicate one by writing it from scratch[2]. Changing the synthesis tool, which swaps the code for logic cells, means re-running the steps that follow: placing and wiring the cells, signoff and physical verification[2]. Foundries also specify which signoff tools their customers must use for tape-out[2].
   - For chips at advanced nodes, 7 nm and below, one vendor holds most of the market inside some steps of the flow. Synopsys alone has more than 90 percent of the market for static timing analysis, the check that every signal arrives in time at the chip's rated speed[2]. In May 2026 SemiAnalysis put Siemens EDA's Calibre at more than 85 percent of physical verification, the final layout check[2]. It put Cadence's Palladium at 55 to 60 percent of hardware emulation[2].
   - Source SemiAnalysis, EDA Market Primer, May 2026, table of tool-level shares at advanced nodes[2]
   - Arm licenses processor designs. In fiscal 2026 it reported $4.92 billion of revenue, $2.61 billion of it royalties[3]. It said Arm-based designs held about 50 percent of the computing power of central processors (CPUs) at the top cloud firms[3].
   - In 2026 Arm also launched the Arm AGI CPU, the first finished chip that Arm sells[3]. By May 2026, servers built on it could be ordered from ASRock, Lenovo, Quanta and Supermicro[3].
   - Design software, simulation software and licensed blocks made up an $18 billion market in 2025, on SemiAnalysis's count[2]. Synopsys, Cadence and Siemens EDA took more than 85 percent of it, counting Ansys as part of Synopsys[2]. No other vendor had more than 5 percent of any of the main categories of design software[2]. Siemens EDA is a US-based subsidiary of the German firm Siemens[16].
   - The two fiscal years do not line up, so any total for this market depends on which twelve months are counted. It also depends on how much of Ansys the total includes, because Ansys sells to other industries too. SemiAnalysis puts 31 percent of Ansys's business in semiconductors and high technology, 22 percent in aerospace and 18 percent in automotive[2]. It counts Synopsys at $8 billion for calendar 2025 with Ansys included[2]. On the same count Cadence is second, at $5.30 billion, and Siemens EDA third, at an estimated $2.2 billion to $2.5 billion[2].
   - Source SemiAnalysis, EDA Market Primer, May 2026, table of calendar 2025 vendor shares[2]
   - Chip firms such as Broadcom build custom accelerators to a customer's design and sell the finished chips. Broadcom booked $16.7 billion of AI semiconductor revenue in the third quarter of fiscal 2026, up 221 percent year over year[4]. That figure covers both its custom AI accelerators and its AI networking chips[4].
   - Before the 2025 letters, a narrower control already applied. Since 14 October 2022, design software specially made for chips with gate-all-around transistors has needed a US export license for national-security reasons[9]. The rule calls that transistor design key to processes at 3 nm and below[9]. That license requirement covers exports to China[24]. The control carried out a December 2021 decision of the Wassenaar Arrangement, a group of countries that coordinate export controls[9].
   - In late May 2025 the US Bureau of Industry and Security, the agency that issues export licenses, told the design-software vendors that sales to China now needed a license[8]. Cadence got its letter on 23 May[8]. The letter required a license to export design software and technology classified 3D991 and 3E991 to any party in China, or to a Chinese military end user anywhere[8]. Synopsys got its letter on 29 May and a second letter lifting the requirement on 2 July[25]. Unlike the 2022 control, the 2025 requirement came from the United States alone and took effect immediately.
   - Synopsys's China revenue fell from 16 percent of its total in fiscal 2024 to 12 percent in fiscal 2025[2]. SemiAnalysis links the decline to tighter export restrictions and to local design-software companies winning share at mature nodes[2]. SemiAnalysis also quoted Synopsys's management saying that when Synopsys cannot sell to a company, that company looks for alternatives, typically local design-software and licensed-block companies[2].
   - In October 2025 CSIS found that only three companies, Cadence, Synopsys and Siemens, make design software that supports the entire chip design flow of more than 40 steps[1]. In a January 2021 report built on 2019 market data, the Center for Security and Emerging Technology (CSET) at Georgetown University found that one Chinese firm, Empyrean, could reportedly run a complete design flow only for some analog and mixed-signal chips[26]. Mixed-signal chips combine analog and digital circuits. For all other chips, Chinese designers then relied on US tools[26]. The report found that other Chinese tools covered only narrow parts of the design flow or supplemented US tools, and largely did not support processes at 14 nm and below[26].
   - Empyrean says it has covered more of the design flow since then. Its 2025 annual report, filed in April 2026, says it now has complete design flows for analog, memory, radio-frequency and flat-panel display circuits[27]. It says it offers 80 percent of the main tools used in digital design[28]. The same report lists foundry certifications for its circuit simulator at 8, 5 and 4 nm and for its physical verification tool, the final layout check, at 28 nm[29]. But CSIS found that as of 2025 Chinese design tools were still far from replacing the full range of advanced capabilities US firms offer, especially for 5 nm and 3 nm chip design[6].
   - China can design accelerators, but it cannot finish one at the leading edge without foreign tools, or build what it designs without foreign equipment. SMIC, China's leading foundry, makes chips for Huawei[5]. In June 2024 CSET found that SMIC's limited 7 nm capacity forced Huawei to choose between making its most advanced phone chips and its AI chips[30].
   - Epoch AI estimates that Huawei trails Nvidia both in what each chip can do and in how many chips it makes[5]. On Epoch's estimates, Huawei's flagship chip for 2026, the Ascend 950, delivers about half the computing performance of Nvidia's H100, which began shipping in 2022[5]. Epoch puts Nvidia's B300 at 6.75 times the 950's theoretical peak performance[5]. Epoch's median estimate is that Huawei will make about 1.5 million Ascend chips in 2026, against 5.9 million for Nvidia[5]. On those estimates, Huawei's 2026 output would carry less than 4 percent of the computing power Nvidia ships that year[5].
   - Epoch puts Huawei's chips three to four years behind Nvidia's until at least 2030, for three reasons[5]. SMIC cannot pack transistors as densely as TSMC, which makes Nvidia's. Chinese memory makers are only starting to produce high-bandwidth memory. And Huawei's software for programming its chips is far less mature than Nvidia's[5].
   + Extreme concentration
   + ·
   + Hard to substitute
   + Chip design turns an idea for a new chip into a complete blueprint. A chip holds billions of transistors, tiny electrical switches. The blueprint fixes where each one sits and how it is wired to the others. The chip runs on a clock, and a signal has to cross those wires within one tick, less than a billionth of a second. Software from three companies, Synopsys, Cadence and Siemens, lays out the blueprint, checks it and signs it off. A foundry, a factory that makes chips for other companies, accepts only designs that have passed that software's checks. The foundry prints each layer of the design onto silicon through a stencil called a mask. Changing the blueprint once the masks are made takes new masks and a few months[1].
   + The US government required a license to sell design software to anyone in China from late May to 2 July 2025[2]. Since October 2022 a narrower requirement has covered software specially made for chips with gate-all-around transistors, a transistor design the rule calls key to processes at 3 nm and below[3].
   + As of 2025, the think tank CSIS found Chinese design tools still far from replacing the full range of advanced capabilities US firms offer, especially for 5 nm and 3 nm chip design[4]. But Chinese design-software companies were winning share at older manufacturing processes, the research firm SemiAnalysis reported[5].
   + Chokepoint card
   + Design is the only stage of the supply chain that happens entirely in software. The US government has restricted it with letters to the companies that sell that software. In late May 2025 the government required a license to sell chip design software to anyone in China[2]. Cadence cut off its Chinese customers until the government lifted the requirement on 2 July[2].
   + Only three complete sets of chip design software exist[6]. A fourth would have to be written from nothing and approved by a foundry[5].
   + world market for design software, simulation software and licensed circuit blocks in 2025[5]. SemiAnalysis put a single respin, a design revision that needs some new masks, at leading-edge nodes at $50-100M in May 2026[5]
   + 90%+ of the timing-signoff market at advanced nodes as of 2026[5]
   + 55-60% of the hardware emulator market at advanced nodes as of 2026[5]
   + 85%+ of physical verification, the final layout check, at advanced nodes as of 2026[5]
   + About 50% of processor compute at the top cloud firms as of May 2026[7]
   + $16.7B of AI semiconductor revenue in the third quarter of fiscal 2026, custom accelerators and networking combined[8]
   + Changing one tool, such as the synthesis tool that turns a design's code into logic cells, means re-running the later steps of the design[5]. The foundry that makes the chip also specifies which signoff tools its customers must use[5].
   + China designs its own AI accelerators, but Huawei's most powerful chip in 2026, the Ascend 950, delivers about half the computing performance of Nvidia's H100 from 2022, on Epoch AI's estimate[9]. CSIS found that as of 2025 Chinese design tools were still far from replacing the full range of advanced capabilities US firms offer, especially for 5 nm and 3 nm chips[4].
   + Synopsys and Cadence, the two largest design-software vendors by 2025 revenue, are American[5].
   + All that checking exists because each revision after tape-out, called a respin, needs at least some new masks[1]. In 2022 SemiAnalysis put a full mask set at more than $1 million at 28 nm and more than $10 million at 7 nm[1]. The nanometer labels name process generations, called nodes, each finer than the last, and no longer measure anything. In 2022 SemiAnalysis also expected 3 nm sets to push into the $40 million range[1]. Its May 2026 primer still puts a leading-edge mask set at $40 million, and a single leading-edge respin at $50 million to $100 million[5].
   + The same 2022 SemiAnalysis article reported that startups had taken chips on TSMC 7 nm, then a leading-edge process, from design to tape-out for $50 million to $75 million, everything included[1].
   + Source SemiAnalysis, The Dark Side of the Semiconductor Design Renaissance, July 2022. The 3 nm figure was a 2022 forecast. SemiAnalysis's May 2026 EDA Market Primer puts a leading-edge mask set at $40M[1]
   + Chip design software is known as electronic design automation, or EDA. Three complete EDA flows exist, each a set of tools that covers every step from code to signoff[6]. SemiAnalysis wrote in May 2026 that no startup can replicate one by writing it from scratch[5]. Changing the synthesis tool, which swaps the code for logic cells, means re-running the steps that follow: placing and wiring the cells, signoff and physical verification[5]. Foundries also specify which signoff tools their customers must use for tape-out[5].
   + For chips at advanced nodes, 7 nm and below, one vendor holds most of the market inside some steps of the flow. Synopsys alone has more than 90 percent of the market for static timing analysis, the check that every signal arrives in time at the chip's rated speed[5]. In May 2026 SemiAnalysis put Siemens EDA's Calibre at more than 85 percent of physical verification, the final layout check[5]. It put Cadence's Palladium at 55 to 60 percent of hardware emulation[5].
   + Source SemiAnalysis, EDA Market Primer, May 2026, table of tool-level shares at advanced nodes[5]
   + Arm licenses processor designs. In fiscal 2026 it reported $4.92 billion of revenue, $2.61 billion of it royalties[7]. It said Arm-based designs held about 50 percent of the computing power of central processors (CPUs) at the top cloud firms[7].
   + In 2026 Arm also launched the Arm AGI CPU, the first finished chip that Arm sells[7]. By May 2026, servers built on it could be ordered from ASRock, Lenovo, Quanta and Supermicro[7].
   + Design software, simulation software and licensed blocks made up an $18 billion market in 2025, on SemiAnalysis's count[5]. Synopsys, Cadence and Siemens EDA took more than 85 percent of it, counting Ansys as part of Synopsys[5]. No other vendor had more than 5 percent of any of the main categories of design software[5]. Siemens EDA is a US-based subsidiary of the German firm Siemens[16].
   + The two fiscal years do not line up, so any total for this market depends on which twelve months are counted. It also depends on how much of Ansys the total includes, because Ansys sells to other industries too. SemiAnalysis puts 31 percent of Ansys's business in semiconductors and high technology, 22 percent in aerospace and 18 percent in automotive[5]. It counts Synopsys at $8 billion for calendar 2025 with Ansys included[5]. On the same count Cadence is second, at $5.30 billion, and Siemens EDA third, at an estimated $2.2 billion to $2.5 billion[5].
   + Source SemiAnalysis, EDA Market Primer, May 2026, table of calendar 2025 vendor shares[5]
   + Chip firms such as Broadcom build custom accelerators to a customer's design and sell the finished chips. Broadcom booked $16.7 billion of AI semiconductor revenue in the third quarter of fiscal 2026, up 221 percent year over year[8]. That figure covers both its custom AI accelerators and its AI networking chips[8].

== /silicon-and-wafers/: 284 lines before, 287 after; as printed only before 38, only after 41; marks out only before 1, only after 4; card 49 lines, the same words; order with the card set aside the same
   sources: 60 lines before, 60 after, the same set, in the new reading order
   the card before: after "See it in 3D in the interactive chapter", before "In plain terms"
   the card after:  after "The world market for silicon wafers was $11.4 billion in 2025, on 12,9", before "How it works"
   words only before: High
   words only after:  High concentration
   words only after:  ·
   words only after:  Moderately hard to substitute
   words only after:  Chokepoint card
   - High
   - Several firms already make 300 mm wafers that fabs have approved, but customers have to test and approve a new wafer plant before they buy from it[1].
   - world wafer market in 2025, on 12,973 million square inches shipped[2]
   - Its parent's electronics materials segment, wafers included, had ¥1,015.7bn of sales in the year to March 2026[3]
   - ¥409,670m of net sales in 2025[4]
   - NT$60.6bn revenue in 2025[5]
   - One of the top five wafer makers by revenue in the first three quarters of 2025[6]
   - €1,346.7m revenue in 2025[7]
   - Losing one of the top five wafer makers leaves four, and a new wafer plant can still be built[6]. Customer approval of a new plant is called qualification[1]. Siltronic completed key customer qualifications at its new Singapore fab in July 2025[1].
   - China made about 93 percent of the world's polysilicon in 2023, and an estimated 98 percent of China's output was solar-grade[8]. Eswin, mainland China's largest maker of 300 mm wafers, says it had about 7.7 percent of world 300 mm shipments in the first half of 2026[9].
   - None of the five largest wafer makers in 2025 was American[6]. GlobalWafers of Taiwan won CHIPS awards of up to $406 million in December 2024 for wafer plants in Texas and Missouri[10].
   - Every chip is built on a wafer, a thin round slice of silicon about 30 centimeters across that carries hundreds of chips at once. Making one starts with quartz rock, which is silicon bound to oxygen[11]. The silicon is refined until metal impurities are no more than a few parts per billion[12]. It is then melted, and a small seed crystal is lowered to the surface and drawn slowly back up. The melt freezes onto the seed as one long cylinder, a single crystal with its atoms lined up in one unbroken pattern from end to end. Saws cut the cylinder into discs, and each disc is polished to a mirror finish and made extremely flat across its whole face[12]. In 2025 the top five wafer makers served around 75 percent of the world market, and none of them is American or Chinese[6].
   - Japanese companies held 53 percent of the world's silicon wafer sales in 2023, by a Fuji Keizai survey that Japan's trade ministry reproduced[13]. Firms based in Taiwan, South Korea and Europe held most of the rest[13]. China's largest maker of 300 mm wafers, Eswin, says it ranked sixth in the world with about 7.7 percent of global 300 mm shipments in the first half of 2026[9]. Chips also need electronic-grade polysilicon, the raw material for wafers[14]. The German wafer maker Siltronic named five major suppliers of it for 2025, and none is Chinese[14].
   - GlobalWafers of Taiwan opened a $3.5 billion plant in Texas in May 2025, the first fully integrated plant for advanced silicon wafers built in the United States in over twenty years[15]. US Presidential Proclamation 11052 sets a minimum import price of $100 per kilogram for polysilicon ingots and wafers from 4 December 2026[16].
   - The world market for silicon wafers was $11.4 billion in 2025, on 12,973 million square inches shipped[2]. That is a small fraction of Nvidia's data center revenue, which reached $193.7 billion in the fiscal year to 25 January 2026[17].
   - In the next step, the Siemens process, the clean gas flows over silicon filaments heated inside a bell jar. On the hot surface the gas breaks apart and its silicon settles onto the filaments. Over days the filaments thicken into gray rods of polysilicon. Everything downstream is made from those rods. Polysilicon comes in two ultra-high-purity grades, one for semiconductors and one for solar cells, and chips use the semiconductor grade[11].
   - A grower melts the polysilicon at about 1,420 degrees Celsius, hotter than lava, and pulls a single crystal out of it by the Czochralski method[12]. The grower lowers a small seed crystal on the end of a rod to the surface of the melt and draws it back up, slowly turning. Silicon freezes onto the seed in the seed's own atomic pattern, and a cylinder, the ingot, grows beneath it. The pull starts with a thin neck so that flaws from the seed's first contact with the melt stay in the neck and never reach the ingot. Boron or phosphorus is stirred into the melt, a step called doping. The added element sets how readily the wafer conducts and whether negative or positive charges carry its current.
   - Epitaxial wafers get one more layer of near-perfect silicon, grown on top at about 1,200 degrees Celsius[12]. That is still hot enough to glow orange.
   - A polished wafer is a doped single crystal polished to a mirror finish[12]. An epitaxial wafer is a polished wafer with a thin layer of single-crystal silicon grown on top[12]. The transistors are built in that layer, so flaws in it can make them leak current. SUMCO says an epitaxial wafer suits customers who need a near-perfect crystal or layers that differ in electrical resistance[12]. SEMI, the industry association, credits the 2025 rise in shipments to AI-driven demand for advanced epitaxial wafers for logic chips and polished wafers for high-bandwidth memory[2].
   - In a silicon-on-insulator (SOI) wafer, a buried layer of oxide, which is glass, separates a thin working layer of silicon from the bulk beneath. Soitec says nearly all SOI wafers sold worldwide are made with its Smart Cut process[18]. Soitec's SOI product lines include RF-SOI for radio chips and fully depleted SOI (FD-SOI)[18]. Its markets run from mobile communications to cloud AI[18]. But for logic chips, SEMI credited the AI-driven growth in 2025 to a different kind of wafer, the advanced epitaxial wafer[2].
   - Source METI, December 2025, from Fuji Keizai data (2023); Japan's and Europe's shares are printed, the others are read off METI's chart[13]
   - Japanese companies held 53 percent of the world's silicon wafer sales in 2023[13]. The chart shows shares of 2023 sales from a survey by Fuji Keizai, a Japanese research firm[13]. Japan's Ministry of Economy, Trade and Industry reproduced the figures in a December 2025 strategy deck[13]. The ministry's chart of the Fuji Keizai survey prints only Japan's 53 percent, so the other shares in the chart above are measured from its bars[13]. An earlier page of the ministry's deck prints Europe's share in the same Fuji Keizai survey as 10 percent[22]. The chart gives no separate share to Chinese or US companies[13].
   - Siltronic's April 2026 investor presentation says the top five wafer makers serve around 75 percent of the market, based on revenue in the first three quarters of 2025[6]. It names the five as Shin-Etsu, SUMCO, GlobalWafers, SK Siltron and Siltronic itself[6]. Siltronic describes itself as the only Western-based maker among the five[6].
   - Shin-Etsu. Shin-Etsu Chemical's electronics materials segment, which includes its wafer business, sold ¥1,015.7 billion in the year to March 2026, up 9 percent[3]. AI-related demand stayed strong and demand in other sectors began to rise, lifting sales of silicon wafers, photoresist and photomask blanks[3]. The segment also sells rare earth magnets and other materials, and the results release does not break out wafer sales[23]. So the figure cannot be compared with the wafer revenue of the other firms listed.
   - SUMCO. Sold ¥409,670 million[24]. Its operating profit fell from ¥36,924 million to ¥1,342 million, and it ended the year with a net loss of ¥11,751 million[24]. In the same year it added leading-edge 300 mm capacity and reorganized production of 200 mm and smaller wafers, where demand stayed weak[4].
   - Siltronic. Lost €77.9 million on €1,346.7 million of sales while investing €369.1 million, with its new Singapore fab as the focus[7].
   - GlobalWafers. NT$60.6 billion of revenue, down 3.24 percent in local currency[5].
   - Worldwide, the wafer area shipped rose 5.8 percent in 2025, but revenue fell 1.2 percent[2]. SEMI attributes the softer revenue mostly to traditional chip uses outside AI, where demand and pricing had yet to improve[2].
   - Polysilicon is the raw material for both wafers and solar cells, and almost all of China's is made for solar cells. In 2023 China made 1.50 million metric tons of polysilicon, about 93 percent of world output[8]. Solar-grade material was an estimated 98 percent of that output and electronic-grade 2 percent[8]. The US Commerce Department found in 2026 that semiconductor-grade polysilicon makes up only 2.4 percent of global production, close to the electronic-grade share of China's output[16]. These figures do not show how much of China's electronic-grade polysilicon ends up in chips.
   - Source USGS Minerals Yearbook, China, 2023[8]
   - Siltronic put the 2025 market for electronic-grade silicon at $1.4 billion, with five major suppliers, none of them Chinese: Wacker, Hemlock, OCI, Tokuyama and Mitsubishi[14].
   - GlobalWafers of Taiwan has expanded its wafer making in the United States. It opened a $3.5 billion plant in Sherman, Texas in May 2025, the first fully integrated plant for advanced silicon wafers built in the United States in over twenty years[15]. The same day it announced another $4 billion for phases three and four in Sherman[15]. The US government had awarded it up to $406 million under the CHIPS incentives program for wafer plants in Texas and Missouri[10].
   - US import rules issued in 2026 on polysilicon products also cover the single-crystal wafers that chips are built on. US Presidential Proclamation 11052, signed 6 August 2026, sets a minimum import price of $100 per kilogram for polysilicon ingots and wafers from 4 December 2026[16]. From the same date it adds an extra 15 percent duty on imported ingots and other listed products made from polysilicon[16]. A September 2026 US Commerce Department rule meant to stop stockpiling before the duties start lists tariff code 3818.00.0040 among the products it covers[25]. That code covers round, doped, single-crystal silicon wafers[26].
   - The US Commerce Department may approve plans to build US polysilicon, ingot, wafer or solar cell plants whose construction starts by 20 January 2029[16]. Firms with approved plans can import production equipment, and polysilicon, ingots, wafers and solar cells in amounts the department sets to match their new investment, without the new duties[16].
   - Eswin, mainland China's largest maker of 300 mm wafers, says it ranked sixth in the world with about 7.7 percent of global 300 mm shipments in the first half of 2026[9]. Its October 2025 Shanghai listing prospectus, drawing on SEMI data, estimates that the five largest makers shipped 79.08 percent of 300 mm wafers in 2024[28]. It puts Eswin at 6.01 percent and all other makers at 14.91 percent[28].
   - Making the smoothest, high-purity 300 mm wafers takes considerable know-how that is hard to write down[20]. Customers have to qualify a new wafer plant before they buy from it, and Siltronic completed key customer qualifications at its new Singapore fab in July 2025[1].
   - The last increase in wafer size, to 300 mm, happened in 2002[30]. Wafers are still produced in diameters of up to 300 mm[2]. Bigger wafers cut the cost of deposition, etching and other steps, because those steps have to be run fewer times for the same number of chips[30]. So printing, or photolithography, takes a bigger share of the processing cost[30]. A 2013 University of Texas lecture cited by CSET put photolithography at half the cost of processing a 300 mm wafer, against a quarter for a 150 mm one[30].
   - Large GPU dies and the newest processes need better wafers. Some GPU dies fill the whole area a lithography tool can print in one exposure. One fatal defect on such a die ruins far more silicon than one on a small chip. Extreme-ultraviolet printing also stays in focus over only a tiny range of heights (see Lithography), so the disc has to be flat at every point. SEMI's wafer group says the move to sub-3nm processes is raising requirements for wafer quality and consistency[2].
   + High concentration
   + ·
   + Moderately hard to substitute

Silicon freezes onto the seed in the seed's own atomic pattern, and a cylinder, the ingot, grows beneath it. The pull starts with a thin neck so that flaws from the seed's first contact with the melt stay in the neck and never reach the ingot. Boron or phosphorus is stirred into the melt, a step called doping. The added element sets how readily the wafer conducts and whether negative or positive charges carry its current.
   + Epitaxial wafers get one more layer of near-perfect silicon, grown on top at about 1,200 degrees Celsius[2]. That is still hot enough to glow orange.
   + A polished wafer is a doped single crystal polished to a mirror finish[2]. An epitaxial wafer is a polished wafer with a thin layer of single-crystal silicon grown on top[2]. The transistors are built in that layer, so flaws in it can make them leak current. SUMCO says an epitaxial wafer suits customers who need a near-perfect crystal or layers that differ in electrical resistance[2]. SEMI, the industry association, credits the 2025 rise in shipments to AI-driven demand for advanced epitaxial wafers for logic chips and polished wafers for high-bandwidth memory[9].
   + In a silicon-on-insulator (SOI) wafer, a buried layer of oxide, which is glass, separates a thin working layer of silicon from the bulk beneath. Soitec says nearly all SOI wafers sold worldwide are made with its Smart Cut process[18]. Soitec's SOI product lines include RF-SOI for radio chips and fully depleted SOI (FD-SOI)[18]. Its markets run from mobile communications to cloud AI[18]. But for logic chips, SEMI credited the AI-driven growth in 2025 to a different kind of wafer, the advanced epitaxial wafer[9].
   + Source METI, December 2025, from Fuji Keizai data (2023); Japan's and Europe's shares are printed, the others are read off METI's chart[4]
   + Japanese companies held 53 percent of the world's silicon wafer sales in 2023[4]. The chart shows shares of 2023 sales from a survey by Fuji Keizai, a Japanese research firm[4]. Japan's Ministry of Economy, Trade and Industry reproduced the figures in a December 2025 strategy deck[4]. The ministry's chart of the Fuji Keizai survey prints only Japan's 53 percent, so the other shares in the chart above are measured from its bars[4]. An earlier page of the ministry's deck prints Europe's share in the same Fuji Keizai survey as 10 percent[22]. The chart gives no separate share to Chinese or US companies[4].
   + Siltronic's April 2026 investor presentation says the top five wafer makers serve around 75 percent of the market, based on revenue in the first three quarters of 2025[3]. It names the five as Shin-Etsu, SUMCO, GlobalWafers, SK Siltron and Siltronic itself[3]. Siltronic describes itself as the only Western-based maker among the five[3].
   + Shin-Etsu. Shin-Etsu Chemical's electronics materials segment, which includes its wafer business, sold ¥1,015.7 billion in the year to March 2026, up 9 percent[12]. AI-related demand stayed strong and demand in other sectors began to rise, lifting sales of silicon wafers, photoresist and photomask blanks[12]. The segment also sells rare earth magnets and other materials, and the results release does not break out wafer sales[23]. So the figure cannot be compared with the wafer revenue of the other firms listed.
   + SUMCO. Sold ¥409,670 million[24]. Its operating profit fell from ¥36,924 million to ¥1,342 million, and it ended the year with a net loss of ¥11,751 million[24]. In the same year it added leading-edge 300 mm capacity and reorganized production of 200 mm and smaller wafers, where demand stayed weak[13].
   + Siltronic. Lost €77.9 million on €1,346.7 million of sales while investing €369.1 million, with its new Singapore fab as the focus[15].
   + GlobalWafers. NT$60.6 billion of revenue, down 3.24 percent in local currency[14].
   + Worldwide, the wafer area shipped rose 5.8 percent in 2025, but revenue fell 1.2 percent[9]. SEMI attributes the softer revenue mostly to traditional chip uses outside AI, where demand and pricing had yet to improve[9].
   + Polysilicon is the raw material for both wafers and solar cells, and almost all of China's is made for solar cells. In 2023 China made 1.50 million metric tons of polysilicon, about 93 percent of world output[16]. Solar-grade material was an estimated 98 percent of that output and electronic-grade 2 percent[16]. The US Commerce Department found in 2026 that semiconductor-grade polysilicon makes up only 2.4 percent of global production, close to the electronic-grade share of China's output[8]. These figures do not show how much of China's electronic-grade polysilicon ends up in chips.
   + Source USGS Minerals Yearbook, China, 2023[16]
   + Siltronic put the 2025 market for electronic-grade silicon at $1.4 billion, with five major suppliers, none of them Chinese: Wacker, Hemlock, OCI, Tokuyama and Mitsubishi[6].
   + GlobalWafers of Taiwan has expanded its wafer making in the United States. It opened a $3.5 billion plant in Sherman, Texas in May 2025, the first fully integrated plant for advanced silicon wafers built in the United States in over twenty years[7]. The same day it announced another $4 billion for phases three and four in Sherman[7]. The US government had awarded it up to $406 million under the CHIPS incentives program for wafer plants in Texas and Missouri[17].
   + US import rules issued in 2026 on polysilicon products also cover the single-crystal wafers that chips are built on. US Presidential Proclamation 11052, signed 6 August 2026, sets a minimum import price of $100 per kilogram for polysilicon ingots and wafers from 4 December 2026[8]. From the same date it adds an extra 15 percent duty on imported ingots and other listed products made from polysilicon[8]. A September 2026 US Commerce Department rule meant to stop stockpiling before the duties start lists tariff code 3818.00.0040 among the products it covers[25]. That code covers round, doped, single-crystal silicon wafers[26].
   + The US Commerce Department may approve plans to build US polysilicon, ingot, wafer or solar cell plants whose construction starts by 20 January 2029[8]. Firms with approved plans can import production equipment, and polysilicon, ingots, wafers and solar cells in amounts the department sets to match their new investment, without the new duties[8].
   + Eswin, mainland China's largest maker of 300 mm wafers, says it ranked sixth in the world with about 7.7 percent of global 300 mm shipments in the first half of 2026[5]. Its October 2025 Shanghai listing prospectus, drawing on SEMI data, estimates that the five largest makers shipped 79.08 percent of 300 mm wafers in 2024[28]. It puts Eswin at 6.01 percent and all other makers at 14.91 percent[28].
   + Making the smoothest, high-purity 300 mm wafers takes considerable know-how that is hard to write down[20]. Customers have to qualify a new wafer plant before they buy from it, and Siltronic completed key customer qualifications at its new Singapore fab in July 2025[11].
   + The last increase in wafer size, to 300 mm, happened in 2002[30]. Wafers are still produced in diameters of up to 300 mm[9]. Bigger wafers cut the cost of deposition, etching and other steps, because those steps have to be run fewer times for the same number of chips[30]. So printing, or photolithography, takes a bigger share of the processing cost[30]. A 2013 University of Texas lecture cited by CSET put photolithography at half the cost of processing a 300 mm wafer, against a quarter for a 150 mm one[30].
   + Large GPU dies and the newest processes need better wafers. Some GPU dies fill the whole area a lithography tool can print in one exposure. One fatal defect on such a die ruins far more silicon than one on a small chip. Extreme-ultraviolet printing also stays in focus over only a tiny range of heights (see Lithography), so the disc has to be flat at every point. SEMI's wafer group says the move to sub-3nm processes is raising requirements for wafer quality and consistency[9].
== /chemicals-gases-and-photoresist/: 331 lines before, 334 after; as printed only before 35, only after 38; marks out only before 1, only after 4; card 49 lines, the same words; order with the card set aside the same
   sources: 98 lines before, 98 after, the same set, in the new reading order
   the card before: after "See it in 3D in the interactive chapter", before "In plain terms"
   the card after:  after "A chip fab draws on hundreds of chemicals, gases and other materials. ", before "How it works"
   words only before: High
   words only after:  High concentration
   words only after:  ·
   words only after:  Moderately hard to substitute
   words only after:  Chokepoint card
   - High
   - This rating is based on photoresist, the hardest input to replace at this stage[1].
   - market for wafer fabrication materials in 2025[2]. SEMI's count includes silicon wafers and photomasks[3]
   - A vehicle of the state-backed Japan Investment Corporation bought 84.36% of JSR in April 2024[4]
   - Resist maker with ¥139,668m of sales in the first half of fiscal 2026[5]
   - €2,465m of electronics revenue in 2025, 9.1% of group sales[6]
   - Bought the polishing-slurry maker CMC Materials in July 2022[7]
   - Says it holds about 65% of the world market for semiconductor sputtering targets as of fiscal 2026[8]
   - Each formula is tuned to the layers it prints and has to pass qualification in the fab[1]. So a new supplier has to develop its version together with the fab, one layer at a time[9]. When Japan required individual export licenses for resist and two other chemicals sold to South Korea in 2019, South Korea bought more hydrogen fluoride from the United States and Taiwan and more resist from Belgium[10].
   - In March 2026 the Chinese resist maker Hubei Dinglong said China had yet to reach large-scale production of advanced resist for 248 nm, 193 nm or extreme-ultraviolet printing[11]. Since August 2023 China has required licenses to export gallium and germanium[12].
   - In photoresist, American companies ranked a distant second to Japanese companies by sales in 2023[13]. In 2021 JSR of Japan agreed to buy Inpria, an Oregon-based US maker of resist for extreme ultraviolet[14].
   - Photoresist is a coating that changes wherever light hits it. A machine spins a coat of it, thinner than a soap bubble, across the wafer. Light shines through a stencil onto the coat, and a liquid wash then takes away the parts the light reached. What remains is a pattern, and the next machine cuts that pattern into the wafer beneath, a step called etching. Hundreds of other chemicals, gases and materials lay down layers, strip them off and clean the wafer between steps[15]. The cleaning chemicals are purified until metal and other impurities are down to parts per trillion, the Japanese chemical maker Sumitomo Chemical said in 2021[16]. A chipmaker has to test and approve each new resist in its own factory before using it[9]. Even a resist that passes the first time takes seven to 17 months to approve, the Semiconductor Industry Association wrote in 2023[9]. Japanese companies held 78 percent of world photoresist sales in 2023, by a Fuji Keizai survey that Japan's trade ministry reproduced[13].
   - In July 2019 Japan began requiring an export license for each contract to sell photoresist, hydrogen fluoride or fluorinated polyimide to South Korea[10]. Japan's hydrogen fluoride exports to South Korea then fell 87.9 percent, but its photoresist exports did not[10]. Production of all three chemicals rose inside South Korea[10].
   - China banned exports of the metals gallium, germanium and antimony to the United States in December 2024[17]. In Europe, germanium metal averaged an estimated $4,100 a kilogram in 2025, against $1,392 in 2023, on Argus Media prices reported by the US Geological Survey[18]. In November 2025 China suspended the ban until 27 November 2026[19].
   - A chip fab draws on hundreds of chemicals, gases and other materials[15]. A shortage of any one of them can stop it. The French gas company Air Liquide alone supplies more than 200 different chemicals and roughly 50,000 cylinders a year[6].
   - Resist makers responded with metal-oxide resists, which are built on clusters with a tin-oxide core and absorb more of the EUV light than chemically amplified resists[22]. In 2021 the Japanese resist maker JSR agreed to buy Inpria, an Oregon-based US maker of these resists[14].
   - The other chemicals and gases put material on the wafer, take it off or keep it clean. Bulk gases such as nitrogen come by pipeline or from plants built at the fab, and specialty gases come in cylinders[6]. Silane gas carries the silicon for new layers into the chamber that holds the wafer. A plasma splits nitrogen trifluoride, and the fluorine it frees scrubs the chamber walls clean between wafers. Hydrofluoric acid cleans the wafer itself. Polishing slurries grind each new layer flat before the next goes on.
   - Japanese companies sell most of the world's photoresist, by two different counts. In a January 2021 brief, Georgetown University's Center for Security and Emerging Technology (CSET) put Japan's share of the semiconductor photoresist market at 90 percent, citing a 2019 estimate[25]. The brief put most of the rest with US and South Korean firms[25]. In a December 2025 strategy presentation, Japan's Ministry of Economy, Trade and Industry (METI) published figures from a separate survey by Fuji Keizai, a Japanese market research firm[13]. That survey counts sales by company nationality and puts Japanese companies at 77 percent in 2021 and 78 percent in 2023, with American and South Korean companies next[13].
   - Source METI, December 2025, from Fuji Keizai data[13]
   - In its 2025 annual report summary, the Chinese firm Hubei Dinglong said China had yet to reach large-scale production of advanced resist for 248 nm, 193 nm or EUV printing[11]. But it said three of its own resists for 248 nm and 193 nm printing had moved into stable volume supply[28].
   - A Japanese state-backed fund, Japan Investment Corporation, took control of JSR in April 2024. A company the fund controls bought 84.36 percent of JSR's shares at ¥4,350 each in an offer to all shareholders that closed on 16 April 2024[4]. JSR's shares were delisted from the Tokyo Stock Exchange on 25 June 2024[29].
   - Two Japanese suppliers tie their growth to demand for AI chips. Tokyo Ohka Kogyo sold ¥139,668 million in the first half of fiscal 2026, up 25.1 percent, as strong demand for AI servers in data centers raised demand for chips[5]. Fujifilm planned to raise its polishing-slurry capacity at Kumamoto in Japan by about 30 percent from January 2025, to meet demand driven by AI chips[30]. It also scheduled new production and quality-evaluation equipment for EUV resist at Shizuoka in Japan and Pyeongtaek in South Korea to start in October 2025[31].
   - World sales of wafer fabrication materials reached $45.8 billion in 2025, and sales of packaging materials reached $27.4 billion[2]. SEMI counts silicon wafers and photomasks among wafer fabrication materials, along with resists, gases, chemicals and polishing materials[3].
   - In electronic gases, CSET named Merck, Air Products and Air Liquide as the market leaders in January 2021[32]. Air Liquide's electronics business sold €2,465 million in 2025, 9.1 percent of group revenue[6]. SK Specialty has made nitrogen trifluoride since 2001, when it became the first company to produce the gas in South Korea[33]. The Japanese firm Kanto Denka Kogyo refines its own fluorine gas and turns it into specialty gases for deposition, etching and cleaning[34]. Stella Chemifa, also Japanese, makes ultra-high-purity hydrofluoric acid for chipmaking[35]. Another Japanese firm, Morita Chemical Industries, sells an electronic-grade hydrofluoric acid[36].
   - A few firms held most of the markets for wet chemicals and polishing slurries in 2019. CSET's January 2021 brief, drawing on a 2019 report by the market research firm Mordor Intelligence, put five firms, KMG Chemicals, BASF, Avantor, Honeywell and Kanto Chemical, at more than 60 percent of the wet chemicals market[32]. Citing a 2019 report by the market research firm Market Reports World, it put DuPont and Cabot Microelectronics at about 56 percent of a $790 million market for polishing slurries[25]. Counting by company nationality, the same Fuji Keizai survey, as published by METI, puts Japanese companies at 51 percent of polishing-slurry sales and 59 percent of high-purity cleaning-liquid sales in 2023[13]. Both shares were 49 percent in 2021[13].
   - The slurry businesses of both leaders CSET named have since moved to other companies. Cabot Microelectronics renamed itself CMC Materials in October 2020[37]. The American firm Entegris completed its purchase of CMC Materials on 6 July 2022[7]. DuPont separated its electronics business into an independent company, Qnity Electronics, on 1 November 2025[38]. Qnity sells polishing slurries and pads[39].
   - In sputtering targets for semiconductors, JX Advanced Metals puts its own share of the world market at about 65 percent as of fiscal 2026[8].
   - In July 2019 Japan put photoresist, hydrogen fluoride and fluorinated polyimide under individual export licenses for South Korea, so each export contract needed its own license[10]. South Korea's chip industry depended heavily on Japan for all three chemicals, and Japanese firms supplied more than 90 percent of South Korea's imports of two of them[10]. Japan's hydrogen fluoride exports to South Korea then fell 87.9 percent[10]. Japanese photoresist exports to South Korea did not fall, possibly in part because Japan allowed three-year bulk licenses for some resist sales in December 2019[10].
   - South Korea bought more hydrogen fluoride from the United States and Taiwan and more photoresist from Belgium[10]. Production of all three chemicals rose inside South Korea[10]. The rise came both from South Korean firms and from Japanese companies' affiliates in South Korea[10].
   - Researchers at Japan's Research Institute of Economy, Trade and Industry (RIETI) found that Japanese hydrogen fluoride exports to the United States rose[10]. They say some Japanese supply may have reached South Korea by way of the United States[10]. Japan ended the individual licenses on 23 March 2023, when it put exports of the three chemicals to South Korea under its bulk export license system[40].
   - For chipmaking materials, China's export controls cover metals. On 3 July 2023 China announced that exports of gallium and germanium would need licenses from 1 August[12]. On 3 December 2024 it said it would in principle grant no licenses to export gallium, germanium, antimony or superhard materials to the United States[17]. On 9 November 2025 it suspended that ban until 27 November 2026[19]. In February 2026 the US Geological Survey put China's share of world production of primary low-purity gallium at 99 percent[43].
   - Most gallium used in the United States goes into chips, and both metals are recovered as byproducts of processing other minerals. Of the gallium used in the United States in 2025, 73 percent went into integrated circuits built on gallium arsenide or gallium nitride[43]. Another 26 percent went into optoelectronic devices such as LEDs and laser diodes[43]. In the United States, germanium goes mainly into fiber optics, infrared optics, semiconductors and solar cells, and radiation detectors[18]. Gallium is recovered mostly as a byproduct of processing bauxite, the ore that aluminum is made from[43]. Germanium is recovered from certain zinc smelter residues and from coal ash[45]. Because both metals are economical to produce only as byproducts, their supply may not rise much when their prices do[45].
   - In Europe, germanium metal averaged an estimated $4,100 a kilogram in 2025, against $1,392 in 2023, on Argus Media prices reported by the US Geological Survey[18]. US imports of the metal fell an estimated 67 percent in 2025 because of China's ban[18]. In October 2024 the US Geological Survey estimated that a total Chinese ban on gallium and germanium exports could cut US GDP by $3.4 billion[46].
   - Source USGS Mineral Commodity Summaries, February 2026 (Argus Media European prices, 2025 estimated)[18]
   - Each resist made without PFAS would need full qualification in the chipmaker's fab[9]. The Semiconductor Industry Association wrote in 2023 that photoresist needs the most rigorous qualification of any chemical a chipmaker uses, because each resist is tuned to the layers it prints[1]. The resist maker adjusts its formula over rounds of tests in the fab[9]. A fab could not qualify all the new resists at once, mainly because testing several together can muddle the yield results[9]. So some would have to be qualified one after another[9].
   + High concentration

== /lithography/: 407 lines before, 410 after; as printed only before 32, only after 35; marks out only before 1, only after 4; card 40 lines, the same words; order with the card set aside the same
