   the card after:  after "ASML sold 327 lithography systems in 2025, 48 of them extreme ultravio", before "How it works"
   words only before: Extreme
   words only after:  Extreme concentration
   words only after:  ·
   words only after:  Very hard to substitute
   words only after:  Chokepoint card
   - Extreme
   - ASML is the only maker of EUV scanners, and Zeiss is its sole supplier of their mirrors[1].
   - for a standard EUV scanner, an estimated $350-400M for the newer High-NA version and roughly $60M for a tool in the DUV segment, according to Reuters in July 2026[2]
   - 22 new chip lithography systems in the year to March 2026[4]
   - 234 chip lithography systems in 2025, 188 of them i-line and 46 KrF[5]
   - A newcomer would first have to make mirrors a meter wide, polished smooth to within tens of picometers, a picometer being a trillionth of a meter[6]. It would also need a light source that hits tin droplets with a laser 50,000 times a second[7].
   - In September 2025 CSIS judged that EUV remained firmly out of reach for China despite massive state investment[8]. SMEE, a Chinese scanner maker, had its largest share in i-line tools, the coarsest kind, and even there held 4 percent of the world market as of 2025[8].
   - No American firm makes scanners, CSET found in 2021[9]. But a US rule published in October 2023 covers immersion scanners with an alignment error between 1.5 nm and 2.4 nm bound for advanced chipmaking if they hold any American-made parts[10]. The US entity list also requires a license to supply the firms it names[11].
   - Lithography prints the pattern of a circuit onto the wafer. A machine holds a stencil of one layer of the circuit up to a light source and projects the image, four times smaller, onto the silicon. The shorter the wavelength of the light, the finer the lines a machine can print. Even so, a machine can print lines narrower than its light's wavelength. Immersion scanners, for example, shine the light through a layer of water between lens and wafer, which lets them print smaller features with the same light[6]. ASML rates its NXT:2150i immersion scanner at features of 38 nanometers or less with 193-nanometer argon fluoride laser light[12]. Air soaks up extreme ultraviolet light, the shortest in use, so machines that print with it work in a vacuum. Only ASML, a Dutch company, makes them. Export controls are government rules on who a company may sell to. Every advanced chip passes through ASML's machines, so export controls on ASML affect all advanced chipmaking.
   - ASML is the only company that makes extreme ultraviolet (EUV) scanners[3]. It also holds above 80 percent of the market for deep ultraviolet (DUV) lithography, which uses light of longer wavelength than EUV[2]. ASML says the production capacity of Zeiss, its sole supplier of optics, limits how many lithography systems it can build[1].
   - Export controls set up by the United States with allies including the Netherlands block EUV scanners from Chinese chip foundries[8]. In December 2025 Reuters reported a Chinese prototype that had made EUV light but had printed no chip[13]. In July 2026 Reuters reported, citing one unnamed source, that a Shanghai firm with two state shareholders has started producing immersion DUV tools, which shine their light through water[14].
   - ASML sold 327 lithography systems in 2025, 48 of them extreme ultraviolet (EUV)[15]. Nobody else makes EUV systems[3].
   - Finer lines need shorter light. Glass and air both absorb the shortest light in use, at 13.5 nm[20]. So an EUV scanner works in a vacuum and uses mirrors instead of lenses, and its masks are mirrors too[20]. Deep ultraviolet (DUV) machines use light with a wavelength more than fourteen times longer[7]. ASML calls its immersion DUV scanners the workhorses of the industry, used in high-volume manufacturing of the most advanced logic and memory chips[21].
   - Deep ultraviolet (DUV) light comes from gas lasers called excimer lasers: 248 nanometers from krypton fluoride (KrF), 193 from argon fluoride (ArF), shone through air or water[7]. Cymer, an independently run ASML business in San Diego, makes these excimer lasers, which generate the light that scanners use to print patterns on wafers[24]. Gigaphoton, a Komatsu company in Japan, also makes excimer lasers for lithography tools[25].
   - Of the systems ASML sold in 2025, 131 were ArF immersion, 78 KrF and 16 ArF dry, all deep ultraviolet, and another 54 were older i-line tools[26]. The i-line tools use 365 nm light from a mercury lamp, and ASML says they print features as small as 220 nm[7].
   - Reuters gives one price for the whole DUV segment, with tools costing roughly $60 million each[2]. ASML's own figures split its DUV sales by tool type. In 2025 the 131 ArF immersion tools it sold made up 42 percent of its system sales, and the 78 KrF tools only 4 percent[26]. In DUV, ASML competes with Nikon and Canon, and it still holds above 80 percent of that market[2].
   - The 13.5 nm light comes from molten tin droplets shot from a generator[7]. A laser hits each droplet twice, first with a low-intensity pulse that flattens it and then with a more powerful one that vaporizes it into plasma[7]. The process repeats 50,000 times a second[7]. Mirrors made by Zeiss, a German optics company, carry the light to the wafer[6]. The largest are a meter across and smooth to within tens of picometers, a picometer being a thousandth of a nanometer[6].
   - Reuters puts a standard EUV tool at around $200 million and a High-NA machine at an estimated $350 to $400 million[2].
   - Source Reuters explainer on ASML's lithography machines, first published 28 January 2026 and updated 28 July 2026[2]
   - ASML sold four High-NA (EXE) systems in 2025, against two in 2024[31]. In its 2025 annual report, filed in February 2026, ASML said it expected High-NA to start supporting high-volume manufacturing in 2027[1].
   - Source ASML 2025 annual report (2024 and 2025); 2023 from ASML's Q4 2023 investor presentation[15]
   - ASML's net sales were EUR 32.7 billion in 2025, at a 52.8 percent gross margin[15]. In October 2026 it expected 2026 net sales of EUR 44 to 45 billion, at a gross margin of around 56 percent[35]. Its 2025 annual report lists 5,100 suppliers[1]. Three of them and ASML's own San Diego site make the main parts of the EUV machine:
   - ASML says the production capacity of Zeiss, its sole supplier of optics, limits how many lithography systems it can build[1].
   - In Japan, Nikon and Canon also make lithography tools. Nikon sold 22 new chip lithography systems in the year to March 2026, in a market it sizes at 570 units for 2025[4]. Its Precision Equipment business made an operating loss in the year to March 2026[39]. Canon sold 234 chip lithography systems in 2025, 188 of them i-line and 46 KrF[5]. Its range runs from KrF scanners to i-line steppers, including some built for advanced packaging[40]. Canon also makes a nanoimprint system, the FPA-1200NZ2C, which stamps the pattern instead of projecting it and can print lines down to 14 nm wide[41]. Canon delivered one in 2024 to the Texas Institute for Electronics, to be used for research and prototypes[41].
   - In January 2021 CSET, a research center at Georgetown University, found that the United States lacked firms in a few key parts of the chip supply chain, above all lithography tools[9].
   - Export controls set up by the United States with allies including the Netherlands block ASML from shipping EUV scanners to Chinese foundries[8]. In September 2025 CSIS judged that EUV remained "firmly out of reach" for China despite massive state investment[8]. For DUV scanners and other tools, the restrictions came in this order:
   - A US rule published on 25 October 2023 covered immersion scanners that print features of 45 nm or smaller with an alignment error between 1.5 nm and 2.4 nm[10]. If such a scanner was bound for advanced chipmaking, the rule put it under US export rules when it held any American content at all[10]. The rule did not apply to scanners first exported from a country that lists them on its own export control list[10].
   - The BIS Affiliates Rule, issued by the US Bureau of Industry and Security, extended entity-list controls to affiliates owned 50 percent or more by listed entities from 29 September 2025[11]. BIS then suspended it until 9 November 2026[45]. Exporting goods covered by US export rules to a firm on the entity list needs a US license[11].
   - The EU added certain Dutch controls to the EU Control List in November 2025[1]. ASML says this changed nothing for its immersion tools, because they already needed a Dutch license[1].
   - On DUV, Reuters reported in July 2026, citing one unnamed source, that Shanghai Aishengna Electronic Technology Group has started producing home-grown immersion DUV tools[14]. The firm was registered in August 2023 with 7 billion yuan of capital and two state shareholders, Shanghai Electric Holding and a Shanghai International Trust subsidiary[14]. It has no website and has taken in teams from SMEE, China's older scanner maker, and the startup Yuliangsheng[14]. SMEE is strongest in i-line, the coarsest kind of lithography tool, and even there holds only four percent of the world market[8].
   - There is less evidence of Chinese progress on EUV. Reuters reported in December 2025 that a Shenzhen team of former ASML engineers had built a prototype that made extreme ultraviolet light but had printed no chip[13]. To print a chip, a machine also needs mirrors that carry that light to the wafer, and ASML's sole supplier of those mirrors is Zeiss[1].
   - If ASML stopped shipping, no fab anywhere could add EUV capacity, because no other company makes EUV scanners[3]. The scanners already in fabs would keep printing only as long as they were serviced. ASML has around 10,000 customer support employees who maintain and upgrade those machines around the clock[46]. Serviced this way, ASML's machines stay in use for a long time. In its 2025 annual report ASML said about 95 percent of the systems it had sold in the previous 30 years were still in use[1]. The same report warned that export controls may limit the service ASML can provide for machines already installed[47].

== /photomasks-and-pellicles/: 275 lines before, 278 after; as printed only before 30, only after 33; marks out only before 1, only after 4; card 49 lines, the same words; order with the card set aside the same
   sources: 68 lines before, 68 after, the same set, in the new reading order
   the card before: after "See it in 3D in the interactive chapter", before "In plain terms"
   the card after:  after "Each EUV layer of an AI accelerator is printed from a mask built on a ", before "How it works"
   words only before: Extreme
   words only after:  Extreme concentration
   words only after:  ·
   words only after:  Hard to substitute
   words only after:  Chokepoint card
   - Extreme
   - AGC said in December 2024 that only three firms supply the blank plates for EUV masks[1].
   - for a leading-edge mask set, SemiAnalysis's May 2026 figure[2]. In July 2022 it put a 7 nm set above $10M[3]
   - Ranks itself No.2 worldwide in EUV mask blanks on its own fiscal 2026 estimates[4]
   - Said in its 2026 report that its share of mask blanks is exceptionally high[5]
   - Launched the world's first tool that inspects patterned EUV masks at the scanner's own wavelength in 2019[6]
   - CSET puts its 2025 share of the market for all electron-beam mask writers at 46.6%, using TechInsights data[7]
   - $849.3M of revenue in fiscal 2025[8]
   - AGC says it spent more than a decade meeting the EUV blank's requirements, such as near-zero thermal expansion, and began mass production only in 2017[1]. Germany's Zeiss calls its AIMS the only system that tests how a mask will print under conditions equivalent to the scanner's[9].
   - As of 2026 SMIC runs two mask shops of its own in Shanghai[10]. No Chinese firm appears in CSET's 2025 market shares for electron-beam mask writers, the machines that draw the pattern onto a mask[7].
   - In December 2025 Photronics called itself a leading worldwide maker of photomasks[8]. As of 2026 KLA sells tools that inspect EUV masks and optical masks, the quartz masks used with deep ultraviolet light[11].
   - A photomask is the stencil that lithography prints from, at least one for each layer of a chip[12]. The same mask prints its layer on wafer after wafer, so one mask shapes millions of chips. The most complicated layers are printed with extreme ultraviolet (EUV) light, and the rest can often be printed with deep ultraviolet light[13]. For deep ultraviolet the mask is a quartz plate that the light shines through. No glass lets EUV light through, so the mask for EUV layers is a mirror. The mirror is built up from about forty pairs of thin layers, and the light bounces off it. Japan's AGC and Hoya are two of the three firms that make these mirrors. A dark pattern drawn on top of the mirror soaks up the light wherever no line should print. Any speck of dust on the mask repeats on every wafer it prints. So a thin membrane on a frame, called a pellicle, sits just above the mask and catches dust where it is too far out of focus to print.
   - On CSET's count from TechInsights data, Austria's IMS Nanofabrication held 46.6 percent of the 2025 world market for electron-beam mask writers, which draw the pattern onto a mask[7]. Hoya says its share of the mask blank market is exceptionally high[5].
   - SemiAnalysis put a full set of masks for a leading-edge chip at $40 million in May 2026[2]. In July 2022 it wrote that more companies will lack the volume to spread the fixed cost of mask sets[3]. On 8 September 2026 ASML and TSMC announced a plan to move from 6-inch to 12-inch masks[14]. They expect the change to raise fab productivity and lower chipmaking costs[14].
   - Each EUV layer of an AI accelerator is printed from a mask built on a blank plate. Only three companies worldwide supply those plates, and Japan's AGC is one of them[1]. Hoya, also Japanese, is another[5]. AGC says it is the only company in the world that takes an EUV blank all the way from the glass to the finished coating[15].
   - On 8 September 2026 ASML and TSMC announced a plan to move from the current 6-inch masks to 12-inch masks[14]. They expect the change to raise fab productivity, lower chipmaking costs and remove stitching constraints, the design limits that come from printing a large chip in two halves[14]. They aim to set up a 12-inch mask pilot line by 2031, so that High-NA scanners using 12-inch masks can enter advanced chip production by 2033[14].
   - AGC says it spent more than a decade meeting the blank's requirements, which include near-zero thermal expansion, perfectly smooth and flat surfaces and no particle contamination[1]. It says it began mass production only in 2017[1].
   - AGC publishes no market share figure for EUV blanks. In its data book, AGC ranks itself No.2 worldwide in EUV blanks, based on its own fiscal 2026 estimates[4]. Hoya says its share of the mask blank market is exceptionally high, and it calls itself the primary supplier for leading-edge EUV nodes[5]. It also says customers may gradually start buying EUV blanks from more than one supplier[5].
   - AGC planned to add EUV blank capacity by 2025, and it says its sales reached their target a year early. In April 2023 AGC said its EUV blank capacity would grow by about 30 percent in 2025[15]. The growth would come from a line in Fukushima[15]. METI, Japan's industry ministry, chose the project for a program that supports new plants and equipment in Japan to strengthen supply chains[15]. AGC also said it was aiming for more than ¥40 billion of sales in the business by 2025[15]. Its EUV blank sales grew 50 percent in 2023, AGC said in December 2024[1]. In February 2025 AGC reported that the sales reached ¥40 billion in 2024, which it called a year ahead of its initial target[21].
   - In September 2023 Intel, the majority owner of Austria's IMS Nanofabrication, called it the established industry leader in the multi-beam mask writing tools that advanced EUV requires[24]. Counting all electron-beam mask writers, CSET, a research center at Georgetown University, put IMS's share of a $994 million world market at 46.6 percent in 2025, using TechInsights data[7]. NuFlare had 39.2 percent and JEOL 14.2 percent, in the same CSET analysis of TechInsights data[7].
   - A mask defect cuts the number of good chips on every wafer that mask prints, so masks are checked as blanks, after patterning and again at the fab. Actinic inspection uses 13.5 nm light, the wavelength of EUV lithography[6]. In 2019 Lasertec launched the ACTIS A150, which it calls the world's first actinic inspection system for patterned EUV masks[6]. In October 2025 it launched the ACTIS A200HiT series, which it says triples the A150's inspection speed[6]. Lasertec reported net sales of ¥230,485 million in the year to June 2026[27].
   - As of 2026 KLA's Teron systems for mask shops detect pattern and particle defects on both EUV masks and optical masks, the quartz masks used with deep ultraviolet light[11].
   - Zeiss makes AIMS EUV, which checks a mask in EUV light under the scanner's own imaging conditions to judge how a defect will affect printing[9]. Zeiss calls AIMS the only system that tests how a mask will print under conditions equivalent to the scanner's[9]. Zeiss says the tool can be extended to ASML's High-NA scanner, the EXE:5000[9]. Zeiss also sells electron-beam repair tools that work on EUV masks[28].
   - A mask set costs more at each newer node, by SemiAnalysis's figures. In July 2022 SemiAnalysis put mask sets above $1 million at 28 nm and above $10 million at 7 nm[3]. It expected 3 nm mask sets to reach the $40 million range[3]. Its May 2026 primer still puts a leading-edge mask set at $40 million[2]. The same primer puts a single respin, remaking a chip after a design flaw, at $50 million to $100 million at leading-edge nodes[2].
   - Mask costs restrict which firms can use a node. SemiAnalysis wrote that at 5 nm and 3 nm, only firms with large wafer volume see the cost per transistor fall[3]. It wrote that more companies will lack the volume to spread the fixed cost of mask sets[3]. It expected failed chip designs to show up first among AI chip startups, which work on much tighter budgets[3].
   - Source SemiAnalysis, The Dark Side of the Semiconductor Design Renaissance, July 2022. SemiAnalysis's May 2026 EDA Market Primer puts a leading-edge mask set at $40M[3]
   - Mask making splits between chipmakers that make masks for their own use and independent merchant firms that sell to them and others[12]. Photronics says that chipmakers' own shops once supplied most of the market, and that production has more recently tended to move toward the independents[12]. TSMC makes masks too, and lists mask making among the services it offers besides making wafers[34].
   - Photronics calls itself a leading worldwide maker of masks for chips and flat panel displays[8]. It had $849.3 million of revenue in fiscal 2025, $615.1 million of it from chip masks[8]. The competitors it names in chip and display masks include Dai Nippon Printing, Hoya, Tekscend Photomask and two Chinese firms, Shenzhen Newway Photomask and Shenzhen Qingyi Photomask[12]. It also competes with chipmakers' own mask shops[12].
   - Chinese and foreign firms make masks in China, but no Chinese firm appears in CSET's 2025 market shares for electron-beam mask writers, the machines that draw the pattern onto a mask[7]. SMIC, a Chinese foundry, runs two in-house mask shops in Shanghai that make masks for deep ultraviolet and older lithography machines[10]. Photronics began operating two mask plants in China, in Hefei and Xiamen, in 2019[12]. Its chip mask business in Xiamen is a joint venture in which Dai Nippon Printing holds 49.99 percent[12].
   - Every EUV mask depends on a handful of firms at each step, from the blank plate to the tool that tests how it will print. Only three firms supply the blank plates for EUV masks[1]. IMS Nanofabrication held 46.6 percent of the 2025 market for electron-beam mask writers and NuFlare 39.2 percent, by CSET's count[7]. In 2019 Lasertec launched what it calls the first tool that inspects patterned EUV masks in EUV light[6]. Zeiss calls its AIMS the only system that tests how a mask will print under conditions equivalent to the scanner's[9].
   + Extreme concentration
   + ·
   + Hard to substitute
   + A photomask is the stencil that lithography prints from, at least one for each layer of a chip[1]. The same mask prints its layer on wafer after wafer, so one mask shapes millions of chips. The most complicated layers are printed with extreme ultraviolet (EUV) light, and the rest can often be printed with deep ultraviolet light[2]. For deep ultraviolet the mask is a quartz plate that the light shines through. No glass lets EUV light through, so the mask for EUV layers is a mirror. The mirror is built up from about forty pairs of thin layers, and the light bounces off it. Japan's AGC and Hoya are two of the three firms that make these mirrors. A dark pattern drawn on top of the mirror soaks up the light wherever no line should print. Any speck of dust on the mask repeats on every wafer it prints. So a thin membrane on a frame, called a pellicle, sits just above the mask and catches dust where it is too far out of focus to print.
   + On CSET's count from TechInsights data, Austria's IMS Nanofabrication held 46.6 percent of the 2025 world market for electron-beam mask writers, which draw the pattern onto a mask[3]. Hoya says its share of the mask blank market is exceptionally high[4].
   + SemiAnalysis put a full set of masks for a leading-edge chip at $40 million in May 2026[5]. In July 2022 it wrote that more companies will lack the volume to spread the fixed cost of mask sets[6]. On 8 September 2026 ASML and TSMC announced a plan to move from 6-inch to 12-inch masks[7]. They expect the change to raise fab productivity and lower chipmaking costs[7].
   + Chokepoint card
   + Each EUV layer of an AI accelerator is printed from a mask built on a blank plate. Only three companies worldwide supply those plates, and Japan's AGC is one of them[8]. Hoya, also Japanese, is another[4]. AGC says it is the only company in the world that takes an EUV blank all the way from the glass to the finished coating[9].
   + AGC said in December 2024 that only three firms supply the blank plates for EUV masks[8].
   + for a leading-edge mask set, SemiAnalysis's May 2026 figure[5]. In July 2022 it put a 7 nm set above $10M[6]
   + Ranks itself No.2 worldwide in EUV mask blanks on its own fiscal 2026 estimates[10]
   + Said in its 2026 report that its share of mask blanks is exceptionally high[4]
   + Launched the world's first tool that inspects patterned EUV masks at the scanner's own wavelength in 2019[11]
   + CSET puts its 2025 share of the market for all electron-beam mask writers at 46.6%, using TechInsights data[3]
   + $849.3M of revenue in fiscal 2025[12]
   + AGC says it spent more than a decade meeting the EUV blank's requirements, such as near-zero thermal expansion, and began mass production only in 2017[8]. Germany's Zeiss calls its AIMS the only system that tests how a mask will print under conditions equivalent to the scanner's[13].
   + As of 2026 SMIC runs two mask shops of its own in Shanghai[14]. No Chinese firm appears in CSET's 2025 market shares for electron-beam mask writers, the machines that draw the pattern onto a mask[3].
   + In December 2025 Photronics called itself a leading worldwide maker of photomasks[12]. As of 2026 KLA sells tools that inspect EUV masks and optical masks, the quartz masks used with deep ultraviolet light[15].
   + On 8 September 2026 ASML and TSMC announced a plan to move from the current 6-inch masks to 12-inch masks[7]. They expect the change to raise fab productivity, lower chipmaking costs and remove stitching constraints, the design limits that come from printing a large chip in two halves[7]. They aim to set up a 12-inch mask pilot line by 2031, so that High-NA scanners using 12-inch masks can enter advanced chip production by 2033[7].
   + AGC says it spent more than a decade meeting the blank's requirements, which include near-zero thermal expansion, perfectly smooth and flat surfaces and no particle contamination[8]. It says it began mass production only in 2017[8].
   + AGC publishes no market share figure for EUV blanks. In its data book, AGC ranks itself No.2 worldwide in EUV blanks, based on its own fiscal 2026 estimates[10]. Hoya says its share of the mask blank market is exceptionally high, and it calls itself the primary supplier for leading-edge EUV nodes[4]. It also says customers may gradually start buying EUV blanks from more than one supplier[4].
   + AGC planned to add EUV blank capacity by 2025, and it says its sales reached their target a year early. In April 2023 AGC said its EUV blank capacity would grow by about 30 percent in 2025[9]. The growth would come from a line in Fukushima[9]. METI, Japan's industry ministry, chose the project for a program that supports new plants and equipment in Japan to strengthen supply chains[9]. AGC also said it was aiming for more than ¥40 billion of sales in the business by 2025[9]. Its EUV blank sales grew 50 percent in 2023, AGC said in December 2024[8]. In February 2025 AGC reported that the sales reached ¥40 billion in 2024, which it called a year ahead of its initial target[21].
   + In September 2023 Intel, the majority owner of Austria's IMS Nanofabrication, called it the established industry leader in the multi-beam mask writing tools that advanced EUV requires[24]. Counting all electron-beam mask writers, CSET, a research center at Georgetown University, put IMS's share of a $994 million world market at 46.6 percent in 2025, using TechInsights data[3]. NuFlare had 39.2 percent and JEOL 14.2 percent, in the same CSET analysis of TechInsights data[3].
   + A mask defect cuts the number of good chips on every wafer that mask prints, so masks are checked as blanks, after patterning and again at the fab. Actinic inspection uses 13.5 nm light, the wavelength of EUV lithography[11]. In 2019 Lasertec launched the ACTIS A150, which it calls the world's first actinic inspection system for patterned EUV masks[11]. In October 2025 it launched the ACTIS A200HiT series, which it says triples the A150's inspection speed[11]. Lasertec reported net sales of ¥230,485 million in the year to June 2026[27].
   + As of 2026 KLA's Teron systems for mask shops detect pattern and particle defects on both EUV masks and optical masks, the quartz masks used with deep ultraviolet light[15].
   + Zeiss makes AIMS EUV, which checks a mask in EUV light under the scanner's own imaging conditions to judge how a defect will affect printing[13]. Zeiss calls AIMS the only system that tests how a mask will print under conditions equivalent to the scanner's[13]. Zeiss says the tool can be extended to ASML's High-NA scanner, the EXE:5000[13]. Zeiss also sells electron-beam repair tools that work on EUV masks[28].
   + A mask set costs more at each newer node, by SemiAnalysis's figures. In July 2022 SemiAnalysis put mask sets above $1 million at 28 nm and above $10 million at 7 nm[6]. It expected 3 nm mask sets to reach the $40 million range[6]. Its May 2026 primer still puts a leading-edge mask set at $40 million[5]. The same primer puts a single respin, remaking a chip after a design flaw, at $50 million to $100 million at leading-edge nodes[5].
   + Mask costs restrict which firms can use a node. SemiAnalysis wrote that at 5 nm and 3 nm, only firms with large wafer volume see the cost per transistor fall[6]. It wrote that more companies will lack the volume to spread the fixed cost of mask sets[6]. It expected failed chip designs to show up first among AI chip startups, which work on much tighter budgets[6].
   + Source SemiAnalysis, The Dark Side of the Semiconductor Design Renaissance, July 2022. SemiAnalysis's May 2026 EDA Market Primer puts a leading-edge mask set at $40M[6]
   + Mask making splits between chipmakers that make masks for their own use and independent merchant firms that sell to them and others[1]. Photronics says that chipmakers' own shops once supplied most of the market, and that production has more recently tended to move toward the independents[1]. TSMC makes masks too, and lists mask making among the services it offers besides making wafers[34].
   + Photronics calls itself a leading worldwide maker of masks for chips and flat panel displays[12]. It had $849.3 million of revenue in fiscal 2025, $615.1 million of it from chip masks[12]. The competitors it names in chip and display masks include Dai Nippon Printing, Hoya, Tekscend Photomask and two Chinese firms, Shenzhen Newway Photomask and Shenzhen Qingyi Photomask[1]. It also competes with chipmakers' own mask shops[1].
   + Chinese and foreign firms make masks in China, but no Chinese firm appears in CSET's 2025 market shares for electron-beam mask writers, the machines that draw the pattern onto a mask[3]. SMIC, a Chinese foundry, runs two in-house mask shops in Shanghai that make masks for deep ultraviolet and older lithography machines[14]. Photronics began operating two mask plants in China, in Hefei and Xiamen, in 2019[1]. Its chip mask business in Xiamen is a joint venture in which Dai Nippon Printing holds 49.99 percent[1].
   + Every EUV mask depends on a handful of firms at each step, from the blank plate to the tool that tests how it will print. Only three firms supply the blank plates for EUV masks[8]. IMS Nanofabrication held 46.6 percent of the 2025 market for electron-beam mask writers and NuFlare 39.2 percent, by CSET's count[3]. In 2019 Lasertec launched what it calls the first tool that inspects patterned EUV masks in EUV light[11]. Zeiss calls its AIMS the only system that tests how a mask will print under conditions equivalent to the scanner's[13].
== /deposition-and-etch/: 380 lines before, 383 after; as printed only before 28, only after 31; marks out only before 1, only after 4; card 49 lines, the same words; order with the card set aside the same
   sources: 96 lines before, 96 after, the same set, in the new reading order
   the card before: after "See it in 3D in the interactive chapter", before "In plain terms"
   the card after:  after "Applied Materials and Lam Research are the two largest deposition and ", before "How it works"
   words only before: High
   words only after:  High concentration
   words only after:  ·
   words only after:  Moderately hard to substitute
   words only after:  Chokepoint card
   - High
   - In 2025 Lam Research held 41.1 percent of dry etch tools, Tokyo Electron 23.4 percent and Applied Materials 17.4 percent, by CSET's count from TechInsights data[1].
   - deposition tool market in 2025, by CSET's count from TechInsights data[2]. Dry etch tools were a $21.9B market in 2025[1]
   - 82.4% of physical vapor deposition tools in 2025, by CSET's count from TechInsights data[3]
   - 51.2% of conductor etch tools in 2025[4]
   - 55.9% of dielectric etch tools in 2025[5]
   - 14.7% of physical vapor deposition tools in 2025[3]
   - Buyers have alternatives[7]. At least five firms sell atomic layer deposition tools, which lay down a film a fraction of a layer of atoms at a time[6].
   - Chinese toolmakers held 12.4 percent of dry etch in 2025, according to CSET's analysis of TechInsights data[1]. They held 2.6 percent of atomic layer deposition that year, by the same count[6].
   - A December 2024 US rule lists some dry etch, atomic layer deposition and deep-hole deposition tools as controlled exports[8]. Its foreign direct product rule reaches some tools built outside the United States[9].
   - Deposition and etch build the chip itself, one layer at a time. Deposition lays down a thin film, sometimes only a few atoms thick. Lithography prints a pattern on it. Etch then eats away everything the pattern does not protect, and the next film goes down on top. Making one chip takes 1,000 to 1,500 steps, the Semiconductor Industry Association estimated in 2025[10]. Some of the holes the etch cuts in memory chips are more than 50 times deeper than they are wide, like a finger-wide shaft about a meter deep[11]. The film has to coat them right to the bottom. The etch has to eat one material and leave the one beside it untouched. Applied Materials and Lam Research in the United States, Tokyo Electron in Japan and ASM International in the Netherlands make most of these machines. Chinese toolmakers have gained share in both kinds of tool since 2019.
   - American companies held 60.3 percent of the world market for deposition tools in 2025, Japanese companies 14.0 percent and China-based companies 9.8 percent, by CSET's count from TechInsights data[2]. In etch and cleaning tools that year, American companies held 52.6 percent, Japanese companies 32.6 percent and China-based companies 10.1 percent[12].
   - Atomic layer deposition tools coat a hole's walls and floor as evenly as its rim[7]. China-based companies held only 2.6 percent of the world market for them in 2025, by CSET's count from TechInsights data[6]. The December 2024 US export rule targets tools for atomic layer deposition of the metals molybdenum and ruthenium, and for coating holes more than 200 times deeper than they are wide[13].
   - Applied Materials and Lam Research are the two largest deposition and etch suppliers. Applied Materials' revenue across all its businesses was $28.368 billion in fiscal 2025[14]. Lam Research's was $23.233 billion in fiscal 2026, the year to June 2026[15].
   - To coat the floor of a deep hole as thickly as its rim, the machine can work in cycles, a method called atomic layer deposition. First one gas settles in a single layer on every surface, and the pump removes the rest. Then a second gas reacts with that layer and nothing else, and the pump empties the chamber again. Each cycle adds a fraction of a layer of atoms wherever the gas reached, so the film grows as evenly on a hole's walls and floor as on its rim[7].
   - The market sizes and leading shares below are 2025 figures from Georgetown University's Center for Security and Emerging Technology (CSET), based on data from the research firm TechInsights. CSET puts the deposition tool market at $26.2 billion[2]. It puts the dry etch market at $21.9 billion[1].
   - Physical vapor deposition (PVD), which knocks atoms off a solid target and lands them on the wafer, was a $4.9 billion market in 2025[3]. Applied Materials held 82.4 percent of it and China's Naura 14.7 percent[3].
   - Atomic layer deposition (ALD) lays down less than one atomic layer at a time. Each step stops once every surface is covered, so it coats vertical walls as evenly as horizontal floors. The US Bureau of Industry and Security (BIS), which writes the export rules, says that even coating makes it important for building 3D structures, such as 3D DRAM, 3D NAND, and the FinFET and gate-all-around transistors in logic chips[7]. ASM International leads it with 54.5 percent of a $4.1 billion market in 2025[6].
   - Conductor etch, which cuts gates, metals and silicon, was a $12.2 billion market in 2025, led by Lam at 51.2 percent, by CSET's count from TechInsights data[4].
   - Dielectric etch, which cuts the insulating oxides and nitrides between the conductors, was an $8.7 billion market in 2025, led by Tokyo Electron at 55.9 percent, by CSET's count from TechInsights data[5].
   - HBM (high-bandwidth memory) needs holes etched straight through the silicon, called through-silicon vias (TSVs), so chips can be stacked and wired[25]. BIS controls the tools that etch these holes at aspect ratios (depth to width) of 10:1 or more and at rates above 7 microns a minute[8]. The control covers only tools that also keep the holes' depth even to within 2 percent across the wafer[8].
   - No one firm dominates deposition and etch the way ASML dominates lithography. In dry etch, CSET's count for 2025 from TechInsights data puts Lam Research at 41.1 percent, Tokyo Electron at 23.4 percent and Applied Materials at 17.4 percent[1]. Tokyo Electron's own results presentation, citing Gartner, gives it 23 percent of dry etch and 27 percent of deposition systems in 2025, a category that includes furnace, ALD and low-pressure CVD tools[28]. Within that category, Tokyo Electron's share of ALD was 15 percent[28]. The same presentation, again citing Gartner, gives it 91 percent of coater/developers in 2025[28]. These machines coat wafers with photoresist and develop the pattern after lithography.
   - CSET's newer analysis, of data that TechInsights published in July 2026, puts China-based suppliers at 9.8 percent of deposition in 2025[2]. They held 10.1 percent of etch and clean that year[12]. In dry etch alone their share was 12.4 percent[1]. In ALD they held 2.6 percent in 2025, most of it from Piotech[6].
   - The Chinese toolmaker AMEC alone held 12.4 percent of dielectric etch in 2025, by CSET's count from TechInsights data[5]. Its revenue was RMB 12.385 billion that year, up about 36.62 percent[36]. It spent RMB 3.744 billion on research and development, 30.2 percent of sales[36].
   - October 2023. A rule issued in October 2023 and effective 17 November rewrote the US control list's entries for chipmaking equipment[40]. It added tools for four processes[7]. The first is dry etch, both sideways (isotropic) and vertical (anisotropic), under ECCN 3B001.c.1[23]. The second is spatial ALD (3B001.d.9), whose design keeps stray particles down to the level advanced chips need[7]. The third is low-fluorine tungsten ALD and CVD (3B001.d.10), which fills deep, narrow gaps in advanced chips with tungsten while keeping resistance low[7]. The fourth is carbon hard mask PECVD (3B001.d.5), which lays down the etch-resistant carbon films that smaller features need[7].
   - December 2024. BIS controlled 24 more types of equipment and three types of software, and added 140 entities to the Entity List[9]. The new equipment entries include TSV etch, and deposition into features with aspect ratios above 200:1[41]. BIS also wrote a foreign direct product rule that reaches some tools built outside the United States[9]. The rule largely exempts foreign-made tools exported by firms located in Japan, the Netherlands and the 31 other countries it lists, whose governments can control these tools themselves[42]. Japan, one of those countries, had added advanced chipmaking equipment to its own control list on 23 July 2023[43].
   - Source CSET analysis of TechInsights data (published July 2026), ETO Chip Explorer[2]
   - Deposition and etch tools are a chokepoint, but a weaker one than lithography, because chipmakers can buy each main tool from more than one maker. In dry etch, Lam Research held 41.1 percent in 2025, Tokyo Electron 23.4 percent and Applied Materials 17.4 percent, by CSET's count[1]. Atomic layer deposition has at least five makers[6].

== /metrology-and-inspection/: 363 lines before, 366 after; as printed only before 34, only after 37; marks out only before 1, only after 4; card 55 lines, the same words; order with the card set aside the same
   sources: 70 lines before, 70 after, the same set, in the new reading order
   the card before: after "See it in 3D in the interactive chapter", before "In plain terms"
   the card after:  after "KLA held more than half of this market in 2025, several times the shar", before "How it works"
   words only before: High
   words only after:  High concentration
   words only after:  ·
   words only after:  Moderately hard to substitute
   words only after:  Chokepoint card
   - High
   - Every kind of measuring and inspecting tool had a second maker in 2025[1].
   - process control tool market in 2025[1]. KLA puts the 2025 wafer equipment market at about $120B[2]
   - 56.8% of process control tools in 2025 by CSET's count[1]
   - 11.2% of process control tools in 2025[1]
   - 6.4% of process control tools in 2025[1]
   - 6.2% of process control tools in 2025[1]
   - 5.9% of process control tools in 2025[1]
   - 4.3% of process control tools in 2025[1]
   - 2.6% of process control tools in 2025[1]
   - Eight firms besides KLA each held more than 1 percent of the market in 2025[1]. But KLA alone held 85.9 percent of defect inspection, the largest kind, that year[1].
   - Chinese firms held 0.7 percent of the world market for tools that measure and inspect wafers in 2025, by CSET's count[1]. Skyverse, a Shenzhen maker of inspection and metrology tools, reported 2,053,298,171.74 yuan of revenue in 2025, up 48.75 percent on 2024[3].
   - In 2025 the American firm KLA was the world's largest maker of metrology and inspection tools[1]. Citing Gartner, KLA puts its 2024 share at 56.5 percent, 6.5 times its nearest rival's share[4].
   - Metrology (measurement) and inspection are a chip factory's quality control. Between the steps that build a chip, another machine looks the wafer over. It checks whether the lines are the right width, whether this layer landed squarely on the last one, and whether a speck of dirt has killed a circuit. The main chip in an AI accelerator, the one that does the calculating, is one of the largest cut from a wafer. A bigger chip is a bigger target for a speck. In a textbook example with the same density of specks, 49.2 percent of chips 1 centimeter on a side come out flawless, but only 11.3 percent of chips 2 centimeters on a side[5]. In 2025 one American firm, KLA, held 56.8 percent of the market for these tools, by CSET's count from TechInsights data[1]. So a chip factory, or fab, cut off from KLA would find fewer of the faults that make its chips fail.
   - By CSET's count from TechInsights data, defect inspection was the largest kind of measuring and inspection tool in 2025, a $6.7 billion market[1]. KLA held 85.9 percent of it[1]. Chinese makers held 0.7 percent of the whole market that year by the same count[1]. Yet China took 26 percent of KLA's revenue in the June 2026 quarter[6].
   - Since October 2022 the US has required a license for any tool subject to US export rules bound for Chinese fabs that make advanced logic or memory chips[7]. A December 2024 rule added specific tools to the export control list, among them inspection tools that can find defects of 21 nm or smaller[8].
   - Process control tools, the machines that measure and inspect wafers and masks, were a $15.7 billion market in 2025, by CSET's count from TechInsights data[1]. CSET is a research center at Georgetown University. KLA puts the whole wafer equipment market, including advanced packaging, at about $120 billion that year[2].
   - KLA held more than half of this market in 2025, several times the share of any rival. CSET's count puts KLA at 56.8 percent and Applied Materials, the next largest, at 11.2 percent[1]. At its March 2026 investor day KLA cited Gartner's figures for 2024, which put its share at 56.5 percent, 6.5 times its nearest competitor's[4]. In April 2026 KLA said its 2025 share was about 7 times the nearest competitor's[9].
   - A bigger chip is a bigger target for the second kind, so the cost of each good chip grows faster than its area[5]. Chipmakers call each chip on a wafer a die. In the standard formula, die yield is (1 + D0A/a) to the power minus a. Here D0 is defects per square centimeter, A is die area, and a is a clustering constant of 3 to 4 that says how much defects bunch together. At a defect density of 0.8 per square centimeter, with a set to 3, a 1 square centimeter die yields 49.2 percent and a 2 by 2 centimeter die 11.3 percent[5]. Because the bigger die yields less and fits fewer times on a wafer, it costs 20.1 times as much as the small one[5].
   - All the market sizes and shares below are CSET's, for 2025, from TechInsights data[1].
   - Source ETO Supply Chain Explorer, CSET[1]
   - Source ETO Supply Chain Explorer, CSET[1]
   - KLA says its share grew 3.6 percentage points from 2021 to 2025[9]. It says it gained share again in 2025 across mask, optical wafer and e-beam inspection[9]. Its revenue for fiscal 2026, the year to June 2026, was $13.579 billion, up from $12.156 billion in fiscal 2025[18].
   - KLA's rivals are far smaller, and none holds more than 11.2 percent, by CSET's count from TechInsights data for 2025[1]. Eight of them each held more than 1 percent of the market that year[1].
   - Lasertec holds 6.4 percent, almost all of it mask inspection[1]. Hitachi High-Tech holds 6.2 percent, most of it from CD-SEMs[1]. ASML holds 5.9 percent through YieldStar overlay and HMI e-beam metrology[1].
   - Nova holds 4.3 percent, through film metrology[1]. It took $880.6 million of revenue in 2025[19].
   - Onto Innovation holds 2.6 percent[1]. It took $1,005.3 million of revenue in 2025[20].
   - Camtek holds 1.6 percent[1]. It took $496.1 million of revenue in 2025[21].
   - KLA's patterning line, which includes several metrology products and mask inspection, grew 61 percent year on year in the June 2026 quarter[6].
   - Source KLA letter to shareholders, Q4 FY2026[6]
   - KLA says the AI build-out is making advanced packaging, and the process control it needs, more important[24]. KLA says it became the largest supplier of process control tools for advanced wafer-level packaging in 2025, adding 14 percentage points of share[9]. It expects about $1.1 billion of packaging process control revenue in 2026, up more than 70 percent[24]. In February 2026 Onto said it had signed a volume purchase agreement estimated at over $240 million with a high-bandwidth memory (HBM) maker[20]. The deal covers 2D inspection and 3D bump metrology, which measures the tiny metal bumps that join stacked chips, through 2027[20].
   - Chinese makers are still small in process control, but one of them is growing fast. CSET's count, built on TechInsights data, puts all Chinese firms at 0.7 percent of the market in 2025[1]. Skyverse (Shenzhen Zhongke Feice), a maker of wafer inspection and metrology tools, reported 2,053,298,171.74 yuan of revenue in 2025[3]. That was up 48.75 percent on 2024[3]. In its 2025 annual report Skyverse says Chinese makers still hold a low share of China's market for these tools and that KLA holds the largest share of it[25]. In the June 2026 quarter China took 26 percent of KLA's revenue and Taiwan 31 percent[6].
   - In October 2022 the US began requiring a license for any item subject to US export rules, process control tools included, bound for certain Chinese fabs[7]. The rule covered fabs that made logic chips at 16/14 nm or below, NAND memory with 128 or more layers, or DRAM at 18 nm half-pitch or below[31].
   - A fab that can buy scanners and etchers but not sensitive inspection tools finds fewer of the defects that lower its yield, so its yield rises more slowly. Most of those tools come from one firm, KLA, which held more than half the process control market in 2025[1]. KLA led four kinds of process control tool that year, by CSET's count[1]. It held 85.9 percent of defect inspection, the largest kind[1]. Still, every kind of process control tool had a second maker that year, by CSET's count from TechInsights data[1]. KLA says its share of the whole market grew from 2021 to 2025[9].
   + High concentration
   + ·
   + Moderately hard to substitute
   + Metrology (measurement) and inspection are a chip factory's quality control. Between the steps that build a chip, another machine looks the wafer over. It checks whether the lines are the right width, whether this layer landed squarely on the last one, and whether a speck of dirt has killed a circuit. The main chip in an AI accelerator, the one that does the calculating, is one of the largest cut from a wafer. A bigger chip is a bigger target for a speck. In a textbook example with the same density of specks, 49.2 percent of chips 1 centimeter on a side come out flawless, but only 11.3 percent of chips 2 centimeters on a side[1]. In 2025 one American firm, KLA, held 56.8 percent of the market for these tools, by CSET's count from TechInsights data[2]. So a chip factory, or fab, cut off from KLA would find fewer of the faults that make its chips fail.
   + By CSET's count from TechInsights data, defect inspection was the largest kind of measuring and inspection tool in 2025, a $6.7 billion market[2]. KLA held 85.9 percent of it[2]. Chinese makers held 0.7 percent of the whole market that year by the same count[2]. Yet China took 26 percent of KLA's revenue in the June 2026 quarter[3].
   + Since October 2022 the US has required a license for any tool subject to US export rules bound for Chinese fabs that make advanced logic or memory chips[4]. A December 2024 rule added specific tools to the export control list, among them inspection tools that can find defects of 21 nm or smaller[5].
   + Chokepoint card
   + Process control tools, the machines that measure and inspect wafers and masks, were a $15.7 billion market in 2025, by CSET's count from TechInsights data[2]. CSET is a research center at Georgetown University. KLA puts the whole wafer equipment market, including advanced packaging, at about $120 billion that year[6].
   + KLA held more than half of this market in 2025, several times the share of any rival. CSET's count puts KLA at 56.8 percent and Applied Materials, the next largest, at 11.2 percent[2]. At its March 2026 investor day KLA cited Gartner's figures for 2024, which put its share at 56.5 percent, 6.5 times its nearest competitor's[7]. In April 2026 KLA said its 2025 share was about 7 times the nearest competitor's[8].
   + Every kind of measuring and inspecting tool had a second maker in 2025[2].
   + process control tool market in 2025[2]. KLA puts the 2025 wafer equipment market at about $120B[6]
   + 56.8% of process control tools in 2025 by CSET's count[2]
   + 11.2% of process control tools in 2025[2]
   + 6.4% of process control tools in 2025[2]
   + 6.2% of process control tools in 2025[2]
   + 5.9% of process control tools in 2025[2]
   + 4.3% of process control tools in 2025[2]
   + 2.6% of process control tools in 2025[2]
   + Eight firms besides KLA each held more than 1 percent of the market in 2025[2]. But KLA alone held 85.9 percent of defect inspection, the largest kind, that year[2].
   + Chinese firms held 0.7 percent of the world market for tools that measure and inspect wafers in 2025, by CSET's count[2]. Skyverse, a Shenzhen maker of inspection and metrology tools, reported 2,053,298,171.74 yuan of revenue in 2025, up 48.75 percent on 2024[9].
   + In 2025 the American firm KLA was the world's largest maker of metrology and inspection tools[2]. Citing Gartner, KLA puts its 2024 share at 56.5 percent, 6.5 times its nearest rival's share[7].
   + A bigger chip is a bigger target for the second kind, so the cost of each good chip grows faster than its area[1]. Chipmakers call each chip on a wafer a die. In the standard formula, die yield is (1 + D0A/a) to the power minus a. Here D0 is defects per square centimeter, A is die area, and a is a clustering constant of 3 to 4 that says how much defects bunch together. At a defect density of 0.8 per square centimeter, with a set to 3, a 1 square centimeter die yields 49.2 percent and a 2 by 2 centimeter die 11.3 percent[1]. Because the bigger die yields less and fits fewer times on a wafer, it costs 20.1 times as much as the small one[1].
   + All the market sizes and shares below are CSET's, for 2025, from TechInsights data[2].
   + Source ETO Supply Chain Explorer, CSET[2]

== /transistors-and-front-end/: 293 lines before, 296 after; as printed only before 26, only after 29; marks out only before 1, only after 4; card 49 lines, the same words; order with the card set aside the same
   sources: 70 lines before, 70 after, the same set, in the new reading order
   the card before: after "See it in 3D in the interactive chapter", before "In plain terms"
   the card after:  after "Nvidia's Blackwell GPU holds 208 billion switches on two dies, each di", before "How it works"
   words only before: Extreme
   words only after:  Extreme concentration
   words only after:  ·
   words only after:  Hard to substitute
   words only after:  Chokepoint card
   - Extreme
   - TSMC, Intel and Samsung all make chips with gate-all-around transistors, the newest kind[1].
   - per 300 mm wafer on TSMC's 4NP process, Epoch AI's December 2025 estimate[2]
   - N2 in volume production since 4Q25, TSMC's first nanosheet node[3]
   - 18A in high-volume production in the United States, as of 2026[4]
   - Started initial production of its 3 nm gate-all-around process in June 2022[5]
   - N+3, a 7 nm-class process made without EUV, SemiAnalysis found in June 2026[6]
   - Said in July 2025 it would begin 2 nm mass production in 2027[7]
   - Rapidus, a newcomer, is building on IBM's 2 nm technology under a December 2022 agreement[8]. Since September 2024 the United States has required a license to share the know-how for building these transistors, except with countries that impose matching controls[9].
   - SMIC makes chips on its N+3 process, which SemiAnalysis measured in June 2026 at 113.4 million transistors per square millimeter, against 107.7 million for TSMC's N6[6]. It builds its tightest layers in several patterning steps because it cannot buy an extreme-ultraviolet scanner[6].
   - In August 2025 Intel called itself the only chipmaker that both develops and manufactures leading-edge logic chips in the United States[10]. It says its 18A process brought gate-all-around transistors and backside power to market together in 2025[11].
   - Intel's 18A process shipped gate-all-around transistors together with backside power, a second power network built underneath them[11]. TSMC says its A14 process will give up to 15 percent more speed or up to 30 percent less power than N2[12]. Rapidus, a state-backed newcomer in Japan, said in July 2025 that it would begin 2 nm mass production in 2027[7].
   - China's SMIC makes 7 nm-class chips without extreme-ultraviolet (EUV) lithography, the most advanced way to print chip patterns[6]. China has announced $43 billion to develop its own EUV[13]. In September 2025 the Center for Strategic and International Studies judged that EUV remains firmly out of reach[13].
   - Nvidia's Blackwell GPU holds 208 billion switches on two dies, each die the largest a lithography machine can print in one shot[14]. Every switch has to turn on fully, let almost no current through when off, and keep doing it for years. Making the switches is called the front end of line. Wiring them together is the back end of line.
   - TSMC's N2, its first nanosheet node, entered volume production in the fourth quarter of 2025[3]. TSMC rates the follow-on N2P at 18 percent more speed at the same power and 36 percent less power at the same speed than its earlier N3E[21]. It gives N2P 1.2 times the logic density of N3E and 1.15 times its chip density, but its technology page does not define either measure[21].
   - Intel and Samsung also make gate-all-around transistors. Intel's version, called RibbonFET, is built into its 18A process, which Intel says is in high-volume production in the United States[4]. An improved version, 18A-P, adds another 9 percent performance at the same power[11]. Samsung got there first, starting initial production of its 3 nm gate-all-around process in June 2022[5]. But SemiAnalysis reported in February 2023 that, because of yield problems, high-volume chips on Samsung's 3 nm gate-all-around technology were expected only in 2024[22].
   - Intel's version, PowerVia, is part of 18A, and Intel calls it an industry first[4]. It cut routed area, the space a block of circuits takes once its wiring is laid out, by 11 percent and dynamic voltage droop tenfold[11]. Droop is the sag in supply voltage when a block of logic switches on at once. TSMC's Super Power Rail connects the backside network directly to each transistor's source and drain, the two ends of its channel[23]. Against N2P, TSMC rates A16, which uses Super Power Rail, at 8 to 10 percent more speed, 15 to 20 percent less power and up to 1.10 times the chip density[21].
   - TSMC plans to start A14 production in 2028, with more than 20 percent higher logic density than N2[12]. It said in April 2026 that two more processes, A13 and A12, are scheduled to enter production in 2029[24]. A13 is a direct shrink of A14 with 6 percent area savings, and A12 adds Super Power Rail to the A14 platform[24].
   - TSMC, Intel and Samsung build gate-all-around nodes with extreme-ultraviolet (EUV) lithography. China's SMIC builds 7 nm-class nodes without it[6].
   - SemiAnalysis reported in September 2026 that Intel's Panther Lake, Samsung's Exynos 2600 and Apple's A20 Pro, which is made on TSMC's N2, all use gate-all-around transistors[1]. In August 2025 Intel called itself the only semiconductor company that both develops and manufactures leading-edge logic chips in the United States[10].
   - In July 2025 Rapidus, a state-backed newcomer in Japan, said it had started making 2 nm prototypes at its plant in Chitose, Hokkaido, and would begin mass production in 2027[7]. Its process builds on IBM's 2 nm technology, which the two firms agreed in December 2022 to develop further for production at Rapidus's plant in Japan[8]. By June 2026 Rapidus had raised 424.95 billion yen in equity[29]. The most recent 150 billion yen came from the Information-Technology Promotion Agency, a state agency under Japan's industry ministry[29]. It has also received government research subsidies since fiscal 2022[29].
   - A node's name, such as 7 nm, does not measure any feature on the chip. Applied Materials, a toolmaker, said in 2022 that chipmakers raise density in two ways, by printing smaller features and by laying out each logic cell more tightly[18]. SMIC's N+3, its third-generation 7 nm process, sets its narrowest wires 32.5 nm apart, tighter than the 36 nm in Intel's Panther Lake chips on 18A[6]. But the 18A process itself allows wires 32 nm apart[6]. SemiAnalysis called the comparison an incomplete, cherry-picked metric[6]. N+3 packs 113.4 million transistors per square millimeter, against 107.7 for TSMC's N6[6].
   - Nvidia's Blackwell is made on an older TSMC process than AMD's MI455X. Blackwell uses a custom TSMC 4NP process[14]. Epoch AI estimated in December 2025 that a 300 mm wafer on that process costs $17,000[2]. AMD's MI455X, launched on 23 July 2026, is made on TSMC's 2 nm and 3 nm processes[31]. It spreads its compute across eight separate dies[32].
   - TSMC, Intel and Samsung do not sell their leading-edge process flows, which set the steps, their order and the tools for each. In September 2024 the United States began requiring a license to share the know-how for building gate-all-around transistors, except with countries that impose matching controls[9].
   - China has money for chipmaking but cannot buy EUV scanners. It launched its third National Integrated Circuit Fund in May 2024 with 344 billion yuan, about $47 billion[34]. China's 7 nm chips have been made on deep-ultraviolet machines, a generation behind EUV[13]. China had stockpiled those machines from ASML, the Dutch scanner maker, before export restrictions took effect[13]. SMIC gets fine features out of them by building its tightest layers in several patterning steps[6].
   - Domestic EUV is further off. China has announced $43 billion to develop it[13]. In September 2025 the Center for Strategic and International Studies (CSIS) judged that EUV remains firmly out of reach despite massive state investment[13].

   + ·
   + Hard to substitute

== /foundries-and-fabs/: 317 lines before, 320 after; as printed only before 31, only after 34; marks out only before 1, only after 4; card 49 lines, the same words; order with the card set aside the same
   sources: 70 lines before, 70 after, the same set, in the new reading order
   the card before: after "See it in 3D in the interactive chapter", before "In plain terms"
   the card after:  after "TSMC calls a plant a GIGAFAB once it can run more than 100,000 wafers ", before "How it works"
   words only before: Extreme
   words only after:  Extreme concentration
   words only after:  ·
   words only after:  Hard to substitute
   words only after:  Chokepoint card
   - Extreme
   - TSMC has not built one of its six largest fabs outside Taiwan[1].
   - over ten years to own a new 2 nm-class fab completed in 2026, BCG's 2023 estimate[2]
   - 79% of its wafer revenue from 7 nm and below, 3Q26[3]
   - $5.8bn segment revenue in 2Q26, only $293m of it from outside customers[4]
   - Over $37bn planned in Texas (CHIPS award, Dec 2024)[5]
   - $9.327bn revenue in 2025[6]
   - Raised 267.6 billion yen in February 2026 to reach 2 nm mass production by 2027[7]
   - In March 2026 the Semiconductor Industry Association (SIA) put the cost to build and equip a leading-edge fab at as much as $20 to $25 billion[8]. SemiAnalysis judged in December 2025 that finding the highly skilled engineers a foundry needs remained a major challenge for TSMC in Arizona[9].
   - Georgetown University's CSET reported in June 2024 that Huawei's Ascend 910B AI chip is believed to be made on SMIC's 7 nm-class N+2 process[10]. But SemiAnalysis judged in April 2025 that most 910B and 910C chips were made at TSMC[11].
   - The US government agreed in August 2025 to buy a passive 9.9 percent stake in Intel, with no board seat[12]. Announcing the deal, Intel called itself the only chipmaker that both develops and manufactures leading-edge logic chips in the United States[12]. Intel Foundry lost $2.089 billion in the second quarter of 2026[13].
   - In 2022 Taiwan held 69 percent of the world's capacity for advanced logic chips, those below 10 nanometers, and the United States almost none[14]. In July 2026 TSMC raised its planned investment in Arizona to $265 billion across 12 facilities[15].
   - The industry association SEMI projected in 2026 that China would keep the world's largest installed fab capacity in 2026 and 2027[16]. China's SMIC cannot buy an EUV scanner, the machine that prints the finest layers, yet it makes 7 nm-class chips by printing those layers in several passes on older deep-ultraviolet scanners[17].
   - TSMC calls a plant a GIGAFAB once it can run more than 100,000 wafers a month, each a 300 mm disc, twelve inches across[18]. It operates six of them, and every one is in Taiwan[1].
   - A new leading-edge fab costs tens of billions of dollars. In 2023 the Boston Consulting Group (BCG) estimated that a new fab for chips at 2 nm and below, completed in 2026, would cost $35 to $43 billion to build, equip and run over ten years[2]. BCG said that was 33 to 66 percent more than the same cost in 2023[2]. Building and equipping alone takes as much as $20 to $25 billion of capital for a leading-edge fab, the Semiconductor Industry Association (SIA) told the US Senate in March 2026[8].
   - Most of TSMC's capacity is in its six largest plants, all of them in Taiwan. Together, the plants TSMC and its subsidiaries run had capacity for more than 17 million wafers a year in 2025, counted as if each were the standard 12-inch size[1]. The six largest plants had a combined capacity of more than 13 million[18]. The other plants are five smaller fabs in Taiwan, two in China, two in the United States and one in Japan[1].
   - TSMC and UMC, both based in Taiwan, make chips only for other companies. TSMC, founded in 1987, pioneered this pure-play model[23]. Chips at 7 nm and below, meaning the newest processes, were 79 percent of TSMC's wafer revenue in the third quarter of 2026[3]. That share split into 33 percent at 3 nm, 28 at 5 nm, 10 at 7 nm and 8 at 2 nm[3]. TSMC's revenue for the quarter was $46.72 billion, at a 66.4 percent gross margin[3].
   - Source TSMC 3Q26 results, filed with the SEC[3]
   - Intel and Samsung design and sell their own chips and rent out capacity too. Renting out capacity is harder for them, for two reasons. Customers dislike handing their designs to a competitor, and each company's own products take its best capacity. Intel Foundry reported $5.8 billion of segment revenue in the second quarter of 2026 and an operating loss of $2.089 billion[13]. Only $293 million of that revenue came from outside customers[4]. Intel says substantially all of its foundry business still makes Intel's own products[4].
   - China's SMIC and Hua Hong and Japan's Rapidus have government backing. SMIC cannot buy an extreme ultraviolet (EUV) scanner, the machine that prints the finest layers[17]. Yet it makes 7 nm-class chips on its N+2 and N+3 processes by printing those layers in several passes on older deep-ultraviolet scanners[17]. SMIC's revenue rose 16.2 percent to $9.327 billion in 2025, at a 21 percent gross margin[6]. Its lines ran 93.5 percent full, on monthly capacity above a million wafers counted in the smaller 8-inch size[6].
   - Two research groups disagree about where Huawei's Ascend AI chips are made. Georgetown University's Center for Security and Emerging Technology (CSET) reported in June 2024 that Huawei's Ascend 910B AI chip was believed to be made on SMIC's N+2 process[10]. It also reported that SMIC's limited 7 nm capacity forced Huawei to choose between its most advanced phone chips and its AI chips[10]. But the research firm SemiAnalysis judged in April 2025 that most Ascend 910B and 910C chips were made on TSMC's 7 nm process[11]. It said Huawei got around the sanctions that barred it from TSMC by buying about $500 million of 7 nm wafers through another company, Sophgo[11].
   - In February 2026 Rapidus raised 267.6 billion yen from the Japanese government and 32 companies to reach 2 nm mass production by 2027[7].
   - In March 2025 TSMC announced plans to add $100 billion to the $65 billion it was already investing in Phoenix, for three more fabs, two packaging plants and an R&D center[27]. In July 2026 it announced another $100 billion for four more fabs at 2 nm or below, taking its planned investment in Arizona to $265 billion across 12 facilities[15].
   - In August 2025 the US government agreed to buy 433.3 million newly issued Intel shares at $20.47 each, or $8.9 billion in all, for a 9.9 percent stake[12]. It would pay with $5.7 billion of CHIPS Act grants that Intel had not yet received and $3.2 billion awarded to Intel under the Secure Enclave program[12]. Intel said it would keep meeting its Secure Enclave obligations and delivering trusted and secure chips to the US Department of Defense[12]. Announcing the deal, Intel's chief executive, Lip-Bu Tan, called Intel the only semiconductor company that both develops and manufactures leading-edge logic chips in the United States[12]. The deal closed on 27 August 2025 and made the government one of Intel's largest stockholders, Intel said in its annual report for 2025[30].
   - The stake is passive, with no board seat[12]. The government agreed to vote with Intel's board on matters that need shareholder approval, with limited exceptions[12]. The deal also gave the government a five-year warrant, a right to buy a further 5 percent of Intel at $20 a share[12]. The government can use that right only if Intel's ownership of its foundry business falls below 51 percent[12].
   - In 2026 Intel began high-volume manufacturing of some of its Panther Lake processors using ASML's High-NA extreme ultraviolet scanners[13]. It also put its 18A-P process into risk production, the trial runs a process goes through before it is signed off[13].
   - In 2022 Samsung broke ground in Taylor, Texas, on a plant with an initial minimum investment of $17 billion, split into $6 billion for buildings and $11 billion for equipment[31]. In December 2024 the US Commerce Department awarded Samsung up to $4.745 billion of CHIPS Act funding, to support more than $37 billion of planned investment in Central Texas[5]. That plan covers two new leading-edge logic fabs and a research fab in Taylor, and an expansion of Samsung's existing plant in Austin[5].
   - In 2022 South Korea and Taiwan held almost all of the world's logic capacity below 10 nm[32]. The United States had almost no capacity below 10 nm that year[32]. In May 2024 SIA and BCG projected that the US share of that capacity would reach 28 percent by 2032[14]. They also projected that new fab investment worldwide would reach around $2.3 trillion from 2024 to 2032[33].
   - China leads the world in capacity for logic chips made at 28 nm or older, but little of its capacity is advanced[34]. Across all nodes, old and new, SEMI forecast in June 2024 that China would have installed capacity of 10.1 million wafers a month in 2025, the most of any region[25]. It forecast 5.8 million for Taiwan, out of a world total of 33.7 million, all counted in the 8-inch size[25]. In the third-quarter 2026 update of its World Fab Forecast, SEMI projected that China would keep the largest installed capacity in 2026 and 2027[16].
   - Political. Taiwan held 69 percent of the world's logic capacity below 10 nm in 2022[14]. In December 2025 SemiAnalysis put Taiwan's share of the world's advanced-node capacity at over 90 percent, though it did not say which node sizes count as advanced[9]. So a blockade or conflict would cut most of that output off from the world market at once. See Who Controls the Chain.
   - Building capacity outside Taiwan is slow, and TSMC has yet to build a gigafab anywhere else[1]. SemiAnalysis wrote in December 2025 that a foundry needs highly skilled engineers to get the most working chips from each wafer[9]. It judged that finding enough workers remained a major challenge for TSMC in Arizona[9].

China's SMIC cannot buy an EUV scanner, the machine that prints the finest layers, yet it makes 7 nm-class chips by printing those layers in several passes on older deep-ultraviolet scanners[4].
   + Chokepoint card
   + TSMC calls a plant a GIGAFAB once it can run more than 100,000 wafers a month, each a 300 mm disc, twelve inches across[5]. It operates six of them, and every one is in Taiwan[6].
   + TSMC has not built one of its six largest fabs outside Taiwan[6].
   + over ten years to own a new 2 nm-class fab completed in 2026, BCG's 2023 estimate[7]
   + 79% of its wafer revenue from 7 nm and below, 3Q26[8]

   + $9.327bn revenue in 2025[11]
   + Raised 267.6 billion yen in February 2026 to reach 2 nm mass production by 2027[12]
   + In March 2026 the Semiconductor Industry Association (SIA) put the cost to build and equip a leading-edge fab at as much as $20 to $25 billion[13]. SemiAnalysis judged in December 2025 that finding the highly skilled engineers a foundry needs remained a major challenge for TSMC in Arizona[14].
   + Georgetown University's CSET reported in June 2024 that Huawei's Ascend 910B AI chip is believed to be made on SMIC's 7 nm-class N+2 process[15]. But SemiAnalysis judged in April 2025 that most 910B and 910C chips were made at TSMC[16].
   + The US government agreed in August 2025 to buy a passive 9.9 percent stake in Intel, with no board seat[17]. Announcing the deal, Intel called itself the only chipmaker that both develops and manufactures leading-edge logic chips in the United States[17]. Intel Foundry lost $2.089 billion in the second quarter of 2026[18].
   + A new leading-edge fab costs tens of billions of dollars. In 2023 the Boston Consulting Group (BCG) estimated that a new fab for chips at 2 nm and below, completed in 2026, would cost $35 to $43 billion to build, equip and run over ten years[7]. BCG said that was 33 to 66 percent more than the same cost in 2023[7]. Building and equipping alone takes as much as $20 to $25 billion of capital for a leading-edge fab, the Semiconductor Industry Association (SIA) told the US Senate in March 2026[13].
   + Most of TSMC's capacity is in its six largest plants, all of them in Taiwan. == /memory-and-hbm/: 281 lines before, 284 after; as printed only before 12, only after 15; marks out only before 1, only after 4; card 43 lines, the same words; order with the card set aside the same
   sources: 48 lines before, 48 after, the same set, in the new reading order
   the card before: after "See it in 3D in the interactive chapter", before "In plain terms"
   the card after:  after "An AI accelerator moves numbers from memory to its arithmetic units an", before "How it works"
   words only before: Extreme
   words only after:  Extreme concentration
   words only after:  ·
   words only after:  Hard to substitute
   words only after:  Chokepoint card
   - Extreme
   - HBM market in 2025, Micron's December 2025 estimate[2]
   - The Institute for Progress estimated in October 2025 that China would make virtually no HBM that year[3]. Huawei said in September 2025 that its Ascend 950 chips would use two HBM designs of its own, without saying who would manufacture the memory[4].
   - Micron is the only American HBM maker[1]. As of October 2026, License Exception HBM lets US and allied exporters ship HBM below 3.3 GB per second per square millimeter without a license, including to China when the chip designer buys it directly and it goes straight to the packaging site[5].
   - Since December 2024, a US license has been required, unless an exception applies, to export to China any HBM moving more than 2 GB per second per square millimeter of its area[6]. The Institute for Progress reported in October 2025 that China's CXMT planned to produce HBM3 in 2026[3]. Even so, working from TrendForce's estimate of world HBM shipments, the institute projected that producers outside China would make 70 times as much HBM as China in 2026[3]. In June 2026 SemiAnalysis judged that CXMT was still struggling to stabilize supply of eight-high HBM3[7].
   - An AI accelerator moves numbers from memory to its arithmetic units and back. The US export control office wrote in December 2024 that a processor cannot reach its full capabilities unless its memory capacity and bandwidth rise with its speed[6].
   - Source Micron prepared remarks, fiscal first quarter 2026, 17 December 2025[2]
   - As HBM grows, memory makers have less factory capacity left for traditional DRAM. In December 2025 Micron put the HBM market at about $35 billion in 2025 and forecast that it would reach around $100 billion in 2028[2]. Micron says HBM needs more wafers than traditional DRAM, the kind used in PCs and phones, to make the same number of bits[14]. SK hynix says the industry's shift of production capacity to HBM has left traditional DRAM undersupplied[1]. The shortage, it says, helped drive a significant price recovery for that DRAM from the third quarter of 2025[1].
   - A US export rule of December 2024 covers every HBM stack that was in production when it was published. The Bureau of Industry and Security (BIS), the US export control office, published the rule on 5 December 2024[6]. It added an export classification, ECCN 3A090.c, for "High bandwidth memory (HBM) having a 'memory bandwidth density' greater than 2 gigabytes per second per square millimeter"[6]. Its technical note defines that density as the memory bandwidth in gigabytes per second divided by the area of the package or stack in square millimeters[6]. The rule also covers stacks made outside the United States, through a foreign direct product rule, which applies US controls to goods made abroad with American technology[6]. BIS stated in the rule that all HBM stacks then in production exceeded that threshold[6].
   - Two provisions narrow the control. License Exception HBM, at 15 CFR 740.25, applies to exporters headquartered in the United States or a Country Group A:5 ally[5]. It lets them ship HBM below 3.3 GB per second per square millimeter without a license, including to Macau and Country Group D:5 destinations such as China[5]. HBM sent to those destinations must be bought directly by the designer of the chip it will be packaged into[5]. Every shipment under the exception must go straight to the packaging site[5]. The rule also excludes co-packaged chips that combine HBM and logic and whose main function is processing, which other chip controls may cover instead[6].
   - China's strongest DRAM maker, CXMT, was still struggling to make HBM in steady supply by June 2026. In December 2024 CSIS called CXMT one of only two Chinese firms with a credible path to producing advanced HBM, though it did not name the other[24]. The Institute for Progress reported in October 2025 that CXMT was planning to produce HBM3 in 2026[3]. It also estimated that China would make virtually no HBM in 2025[3]. In June 2026 SemiAnalysis judged that CXMT was still struggling to stabilize supply of eight-high HBM3, with even greater challenges at twelve-high[7].
   - Huawei plans to use HBM of its own design in its Ascend 950 AI chips, but when it announced the designs in September 2025 it did not say who would make the memory[4]. The two designs are called HiBL 1.0 and HiZQ 2.0[4]. Huawei said the 950DT, due in the fourth quarter of 2026, would carry 144 GB of HiZQ 2.0 at 4 TB/s[4].
   + Extreme concentration
   + ·
   + Hard to substitute
