== /advanced-packaging/: 334 lines before, 337 after; as printed only before 36, only after 39; marks out only before 1, only after 4; card 52 lines, the same words; order with the card set aside the same
   sources: 66 lines before, 66 after, the same set, in the new reading order
   the card before: after "See it in 3D in the interactive chapter", before "In plain terms"
   the card after:  after "A lithography scanner prints a rectangle 26 mm by 33 mm, or 858 mm2, i", before "How it works"
   words only before: Extreme
   words only after:  Extreme concentration
   words only after:  ·
   words only after:  Moderately hard to substitute
   words only after:  Chokepoint card
   - Extreme
   - Rated on CoWoS capacity, which TSMC said in July 2026 was so tight that it limited its customers' growth[1].
   - for the CoWoS-L packaging of one Nvidia B200, against about $900 for its two logic dies, by Epoch AI's December 2025 estimate[2]
   - CoWoS packaging used by Nvidia, Google, AMD and Amazon in 2025[3]
   - Foveros 3D packaging in volume at Rio Rancho, New Mexico, since January 2024[4]
   - NT$389.2B assembly, test and materials revenue, 2025[5]
   - $6.71B net sales, 2025[6]
   - RMB 38.87B revenue, 2025[7]
   - Thermocompression bonders, with $532.1M of advanced packaging revenue in 2025[8]
   - CoWoS packaging has been outsourced to assembly firms such as ASE and Amkor before[9]. Intel's EMIB, which links chips through silicon bridges embedded in the substrate, is another option, and Amazon's Trainium and Google's TPU were both adopting it as of March 2026[9]. But a newcomer would have to match TSMC, which said in April 2026 that it was producing CoWoS at 5.5 times the area a scanner prints in one shot[10].
   - Chinese firms had 5.3 percent of the market for fabrication tools built for advanced packaging in 2025, by CSET's analysis of TechInsights data[11]. Between 2019 and 2024 they gained 5 percentage points of share in test tools but lost share in bonding and other conventional assembly and packaging tools[12].
   - Intel opened Fab 9 in Rio Rancho, New Mexico, in January 2024 to make its Foveros 3D packages in volume[4]. Amkor broke ground in October 2025 on a $7B packaging and test campus in Peoria, Arizona, and expects production to start in early 2028[13].
   - Epoch AI estimates that in 2025 Nvidia used 60.3 percent of all capacity for CoWoS, TSMC's interposer packaging[3]. On the July 2026 earnings call, TSMC's chief executive, C.C. Wei, said its packaging capacity was so tight that it limited customers' growth[1]. Asked about Intel's EMIB-T packaging, he said he hoped it would take some of the load off TSMC[1].
   - In a TrendForce ranking by 2024 revenue, which CSET cites, four of the top ten outsourced firms that package and test chips are headquartered in China[12]. But in 2025 Chinese firms had 5.3 percent of the market for advanced packaging fabrication tools, against 65.8 percent for US firms, by CSET's analysis of TechInsights data[11].
   - A lithography scanner prints a rectangle 26 mm by 33 mm, or 858 mm2, in one exposure[14]. That area is called the reticle field. Every leading AI accelerator, counting its memory stacks, covers more area than that. Packaging is the stage that lets an accelerator be larger than one reticle field, and the packaging method limits how much larger it can be.
   - A second reason for the interposer is high bandwidth memory (HBM). An HBM4 stack connects through 2,048 data signals, packed too tightly for a standard resin substrate, so the memory sits beside the logic on the interposer[15]. Even Nvidia's H100, whose single logic die is 814 mm2 and fits inside one reticle field, carries five HBM stacks[16]. Epoch AI counts TSMC's CoWoS packaging among the three inputs the H100 needs, along with its logic die and its HBM[3].
   - One cracked joint can scrap a package holding eight tested HBM stacks and two large, expensive logic dies. Epoch AI estimated in December 2025 that CoWoS-L packaging, the version built with small silicon bridges, added about $1,100 to the cost of building one Nvidia B200[2]. That is more than its estimate of about $900 for the B200's two logic dies[2].
   - Intel's EMIB embeds similar silicon bridges in the resin substrate[17]. SemiAnalysis reported in March 2026 that EMIB was gaining traction, with Amazon's Trainium and Google's TPU both adopting it[9].
   - TSMC put SoIC into volume production in 2022, stacking SRAM memory chips on logic wafers[23]. Its 3 nm SoIC entered volume production in 2025[19]. An A14-on-A14 version, stacking dies made on TSMC's 1.4 nm process, is due in 2029[10]. It will carry 1.8 times the die-to-die connection density of SoIC that stacks 2 nm dies on 2 nm dies[10].
   - SoIC bonding also brings optics into the package. TSMC's COUPE uses SoIC to join a silicon photonics chip, which sends data as light, to its electrical control chip[19]. TSMC said in April 2026 that COUPE on substrate would begin production in 2026[10]. It also said that putting COUPE inside the package gives twice the power efficiency of a pluggable version on the circuit board[10].
   - TSMC does not publish its CoWoS capacity[3]. Industry estimates from SemiWiki and TrendForce, compiled by Epoch AI, put it at about 65,000 to 75,000 wafers a month at the end of 2025, up from 35,000 to 40,000 a year earlier[3].
   - By Epoch AI's estimate, four chip designers used most of the world's CoWoS capacity in 2025 but only a small share of its advanced chipmaking. Epoch AI compared each chip designer's use of components with world supply[3]. It puts Nvidia at 60.3 percent of all CoWoS capacity in 2025, with a 90 percent confidence interval of 56.5 to 64.3[3]. Google took 13.5 percent, AMD 8.4 and Amazon 7.4[3]. Together those four used around 90 percent of global CoWoS capacity and HBM supply, with HBM measured by value[3]. But they used only 12 percent of advanced logic die production[3].
   - Source Epoch AI, advanced packaging and HBM were the bottlenecks on AI chip production in 2025[3]
   - In March 2026 the research firm SemiAnalysis judged that front-end capacity had become the dominant bottleneck and that CoWoS constraints were easing[9]. It said TSMC was planning CoWoS capacity around how many wafers N3, its 3 nm process, could produce, because added packaging capacity is of no use without front-end wafers to fill it[9]. It also noted that CoWoS packaging can be, and has been, outsourced to assembly firms such as ASE and Amkor[9].
   - TSMC gave a different account four months later. On the July 2026 earnings call, Wei said TSMC's packaging capacity was so tight that it limited customers' growth[1]. Asked about Intel's EMIB-T, he said he welcomed the extra flexibility and hoped it would take some of the load off TSMC[1]. Later in the call he said back-end capacity was still in shortage[26].
   - Source TSMC 2025 annual report and 2026 technology symposium[10]
   - Three kinds of firm package chips. TSMC, which makes chips for others, and Intel, which makes its own, do it in their own back-end fabs. Outsourced assembly and test firms do the rest. Intel opened Fab 9 in Rio Rancho, New Mexico, in January 2024 to make its Foveros 3D packages in volume[4]. It called the plant the only US factory producing the most advanced packaging at scale[4].
   - ASE leads the outsourced assembly and test firms, and Amkor is the largest one headquartered in the United States. ASE Technology Holding had revenue of NT$645.4 billion in 2025, about 40 percent of it from its electronics manufacturing arm, a business separate from assembly and test[5]. Its assembly, test and materials business brought in NT$389.2 billion, up from NT$325.9 billion in 2024[5]. Amkor had net sales of $6.71 billion in 2025[6].
   - Thermocompression bonding. Pressing dies together under heat and force. ASMPT's advanced packaging revenue reached $532.1 million in 2025, with thermocompression bonder revenue up about 146 percent[8]. ASMPT targets 35 to 40 percent of a market it puts at $1.6 billion in 2028[8].
   - TSMC and Amkor both plan advanced packaging plants in Arizona. TSMC's Arizona commitment reached $265 billion in July 2026 and covers two advanced packaging plants alongside 10 fabs[29]. Amkor started building a $7 billion campus in Peoria, Arizona, in October 2025 and expects production from early 2028[13]. The US Commerce Department awarded Amkor up to $407 million in CHIPS Act funding for the site in December 2024[30]. In October 2025 Wei said a large outsourced packaging partner, which he did not name, was already breaking ground in Arizona[31]. He said its plant was on an earlier schedule than TSMC's own two packaging fabs there[31].
   - Source CSET, Inside Beijing’s Chipmaking Offensive[12]
   - Four of the top ten outsourced assembly and test firms by 2024 revenue are headquartered in China, by a TrendForce ranking that CSET cites[12]. JCET, a leading Chinese assembly and test firm, had record revenue of RMB 38.87 billion in 2025, up 8.1 percent[7]. JCET says its XDFOI packaging platform has entered high-volume manufacturing with redistribution-layer interposer, silicon interposer and silicon bridge options[32]. Those are the same three kinds of carrier that TSMC sells as CoWoS-R, CoWoS-S and CoWoS-L.
   - Chinese firms had a small share of the market for advanced packaging tools in 2024 and 2025, and they lost ground in bonding. In 2024 Chinese firms had 7 percent of the market for advanced packaging fabrication tools[12]. Between 2019 and 2024, they gained 5 percentage points of share in test tools and 4 points in fabrication tools built for advanced packaging, by CSET's analysis of TechInsights data[12]. But they lost share in bonding and other conventional assembly and packaging tools, the category where CSET counts hybrid and thermocompression bonders[12]. Within the advanced packaging fabrication tools, Chinese firms gained 10 points in etch and clean tools and 3 points in deposition tools[12]. They lost 2 points each in lithography and polishing (CMP) tools[12]. CSET's Chip Explorer, a separate data tool also built on TechInsights data, puts Chinese firms at 5.3 percent of the $6.2 billion world market for advanced packaging fabrication tools in 2025[11]. It puts US firms at 65.8 percent and Japanese firms at 15.3 percent[11].
   - TSMC said in April 2026 that it was producing CoWoS at 5.5 times the size of a reticle field and planning larger versions[10]. Each generation on TSMC's roadmap needs a larger interposer.
   - 2028. A 14-reticle interposer, about the area of a compact disc, carrying about ten large compute dies and 20 HBM stacks[10].
   - 2029. More than 14 reticles, plus a 40-reticle System-on-Wafer[10]. TSMC describes System-on-Wafer as joining different data center chips at the scale of a whole wafer[33].
   + Extreme concentration
   + ·
   + Moderately hard to substitute
   + Epoch AI estimates that in 2025 Nvidia used 60.3 percent of all capacity for CoWoS, TSMC's interposer packaging[1]. On the July 2026 earnings call, TSMC's chief executive, C.C. Wei, said its packaging capacity was so tight that it limited customers' growth[2]. Asked about Intel's EMIB-T packaging, he said he hoped it would take some of the load off TSMC[2].
   + In a TrendForce ranking by 2024 revenue, which CSET cites, four of the top ten outsourced firms that package and test chips are headquartered in China[3]. But in 2025 Chinese firms had 5.3 percent of the market for advanced packaging fabrication tools, against 65.8 percent for US firms, by CSET's analysis of TechInsights data[4].
   + Chokepoint card
   + A lithography scanner prints a rectangle 26 mm by 33 mm, or 858 mm2, in one exposure[5]. That area is called the reticle field. Every leading AI accelerator, counting its memory stacks, covers more area than that. Packaging is the stage that lets an accelerator be larger than one reticle field, and the packaging method limits how much larger it can be.
   + Rated on CoWoS capacity, which TSMC said in July 2026 was so tight that it limited its customers' growth[2].
   + for the CoWoS-L packaging of one Nvidia B200, against about $900 for its two logic dies, by Epoch AI's December 2025 estimate[6]
   + CoWoS packaging used by Nvidia, Google, AMD and Amazon in 2025[1]
   + Foveros 3D packaging in volume at Rio Rancho, New Mexico, since January 2024[7]
   + NT$389.2B assembly, test and materials revenue, 2025[8]
   + $6.71B net sales, 2025[9]
   + RMB 38.87B revenue, 2025[10]
   + Thermocompression bonders, with $532.1M of advanced packaging revenue in 2025[11]
   + CoWoS packaging has been outsourced to assembly firms such as ASE and Amkor before[12]. Intel's EMIB, which links chips through silicon bridges embedded in the substrate, is another option, and Amazon's Trainium and Google's TPU were both adopting it as of March 2026[12]. But a newcomer would have to match TSMC, which said in April 2026 that it was producing CoWoS at 5.5 times the area a scanner prints in one shot[13].
   + Chinese firms had 5.3 percent of the market for fabrication tools built for advanced packaging in 2025, by CSET's analysis of TechInsights data[4]. Between 2019 and 2024 they gained 5 percentage points of share in test tools but lost share in bonding and other conventional assembly and packaging tools[3].
   + Intel opened Fab 9 in Rio Rancho, New Mexico, in January 2024 to make its Foveros 3D packages in volume[7]. Amkor broke ground in October 2025 on a $7B packaging and test campus in Peoria, Arizona, and expects production to start in early 2028[14].
   + A second reason for the interposer is high bandwidth memory (HBM). An HBM4 stack connects through 2,048 data signals, packed too tightly for a standard resin substrate, so the memory sits beside the logic on the interposer[15]. Even Nvidia's H100, whose single logic die is 814 mm2 and fits inside one reticle field, carries five HBM stacks[16]. Epoch AI counts TSMC's CoWoS packaging among the three inputs the H100 needs, along with its logic die and its HBM[1].
   + One cracked joint can scrap a package holding eight tested HBM stacks and two large, expensive logic dies. Epoch AI estimated in December 2025 that CoWoS-L packaging, the version built with small silicon bridges, added about $1,100 to the cost of building one Nvidia B200[6]. That is more than its estimate of about $900 for the B200's two logic dies[6].
   + Intel's EMIB embeds similar silicon bridges in the resin substrate[17]. SemiAnalysis reported in March 2026 that EMIB was gaining traction, with Amazon's Trainium and Google's TPU both adopting it[12].
   + TSMC put SoIC into volume production in 2022, stacking SRAM memory chips on logic wafers[23]. Its 3 nm SoIC entered volume production in 2025[19]. An A14-on-A14 version, stacking dies made on TSMC's 1.4 nm process, is due in 2029[13]. It will carry 1.8 times the die-to-die connection density of SoIC that stacks 2 nm dies on 2 nm dies[13].
   + SoIC bonding also brings optics into the package. TSMC's COUPE uses SoIC to join a silicon photonics chip, which sends data as light, to its electrical control chip[19]. TSMC said in April 2026 that COUPE on substrate would begin production in 2026[13]. It also said that putting COUPE inside the package gives twice the power efficiency of a pluggable version on the circuit board[13].
   + TSMC does not publish its CoWoS capacity[1]. Industry estimates from SemiWiki and TrendForce, compiled by Epoch AI, put it at about 65,000 to 75,000 wafers a month at the end of 2025, up from 35,000 to 40,000 a year earlier[1].
   + By Epoch AI's estimate, four chip designers used most of the world's CoWoS capacity in 2025 but only a small share of its advanced chipmaking. Epoch AI compared each chip designer's use of components with world supply[1]. It puts Nvidia at 60.3 percent of all CoWoS capacity in 2025, with a 90 percent confidence interval of 56.5 to 64.3[1]. Google took 13.5 percent, AMD 8.4 and Amazon 7.4[1]. Together those four used around 90 percent of global CoWoS capacity and HBM supply, with HBM measured by value[1]. But they used only 12 percent of advanced logic die production[1].
   + Source Epoch AI, advanced packaging and HBM were the bottlenecks on AI chip production in 2025[1]
   + In March 2026 the research firm SemiAnalysis judged that front-end capacity had become the dominant bottleneck and that CoWoS constraints were easing[12]. It also noted that CoWoS packaging can be, and has been, outsourced to assembly firms such as ASE and Amkor[12].

== /substrates-and-pcbs/: 327 lines before, 330 after; as printed only before 30, only after 33; marks out only before 1, only after 4; card 49 lines, the same words; order with the card set aside the same
   sources: 94 lines before, 94 after, the same set, in the new reading order
   the card before: after "See it in 3D in the interactive chapter", before "In plain terms"
   the card after:  after "The silicon interposer wires chips together inside a package. Beyond t", before "How it works"
   words only before: Extreme
   words only after:  Extreme concentration
   words only after:  ·
   words only after:  Hard to substitute
   words only after:  Chokepoint card
   - Extreme
   - Rated on the insulating film inside the substrate, because chipmakers and device makers would have to approve any rival film before substrate makers could use it[1].
   - market for FC-BGA substrates in 2025, up 18 percent, in a Prismark estimate cited by AT&S[2]
   - Told investors in June 2026 that its build-up film has held over 95% of its market since launch[3]
   - Claimed an overwhelming advantage in low thermal expansion glass in its 2025 integrated report[4]
   - Said in May 2026 that it typically takes close to 100% of each new generation of interposer-type substrates at launch[5]
   - Build-up substrates were 52% of sales in Q2 2026[6]
   - Package solution sales KRW 771.6bn in Q2 2026, up 37%[7]
   - Ajinomoto makes the thin insulating film between the substrate's wiring layers[8]. Chipmakers and device makers approve substrate materials such as laminate, so a rival film would need their approval too[1].
   - TTM, an American circuit board maker, said in its annual report for 2025 that its circuit board competitors are mostly based in China and Taiwan[9]. Nitto Boseki, a Japanese glass fiber maker, said in its 2025 integrated report that Chinese and Taiwanese companies are aggressively catching up with it in the special glass sector[4].
   - The White House's 2021 supply chain review listed no American firm among the key substrate makers[10]. In December 2024 the US Commerce Department finalized an award of up to $75 million to Absolics, an affiliate of Korea's SKC, for a glass substrate plant in Covington, Georgia[11].
   - Ajinomoto's third site for making its build-up film, in Gifu Prefecture, is scheduled to start operating in 2032 to meet demand after 2030[12]. Nitto Boseki says market demand seems to have risen about two to three times since it set its plan for fiscal 2024 to 2027[4]. In January 2026 Nikkei Asia reported that Apple and Qualcomm were among the customers worried about Japanese glass cloth supply[13]. In August 2026 Ibiden said it had prospects of a third and fourth glass cloth supplier and expected its material procurement risks to decrease[14].
   - The silicon interposer wires chips together inside a package. Beyond the interposer, the wiring runs through a build-up substrate made of epoxy and glass, then a printed circuit board that can have more than 30 layers, then a server rack[9].
   - One company, Ajinomoto, supplies nearly all the insulating film inside package substrates for logic chips. Ajinomoto Build-up Film, or ABF, is the insulating film between the layers of package substrates for logic chips, used in PCs, network equipment, servers and AI equipment[8]. Ajinomoto first developed it in 1999 and says major chipmakers have used it as the de facto standard ever since[22]. On Ajinomoto's own site, its electronic materials division said practically all high-performance computers and servers rely on the film[16]. In June 2026 Ajinomoto told investors that the film has held a share of over 95 percent since its launch[3].
   - Absolics, a subsidiary of Korea's SKC, has a final US Commerce Department award of up to $75 million for a 120,000-square-foot glass substrate plant in Covington, Georgia[24]. The department finalized the award in December 2024[11]. As of October 2026, the award's project page at NIST, a Commerce Department agency, still said first deliveries to customers were expected in 2025 and production capacity in 2027, without saying whether those deliveries had happened[24].
   - Ajinomoto's electronic materials revenue is rising, and it is adding film capacity, but its third production site is not scheduled to open until 2032. Ajinomoto's Healthcare and Others segment, which includes electronic materials such as the film, lifted business profit 45.1 percent to JPY 66.2 billion in the year to March 2026[27]. Ajinomoto attributes the rise mainly to higher electronic materials revenue and higher profit in Bio-Pharma Services & Ingredients[27]. It has set an investment budget of JPY 25 billion between 2023 and 2030 to expand film production[28]. It says it has no concerns about its current supply chain overall and will steadily expand its supply capacity over the medium to long term[3]. In May 2026 it announced the acquisition of land in Gifu Prefecture for a third production site, scheduled to start operating in 2032 to meet demand after 2030[12].
   - Nitto Boseki says market demand seems to have risen about two to three times since it set its plan for fiscal 2024 to 2027[4]. That plan included about JPY 80 billion of capital spending[31]. The company claims an overwhelming advantage in quality and capacity for low-expansion glass[4]. In August 2025 it decided to expand glass fiber cloth production at its Fukushima Enterprise Center, with production scheduled to begin in the fourth quarter of its fiscal 2026[32]. In May 2026 it forecast capital investment of JPY 45,000 million for the year to March 2027, up from JPY 21,702 million in the year to March 2026[33].
   - In January 2026 Nikkei Asia reported that Apple and Qualcomm were among the customers worried about Japanese glass cloth supply[13]. In August 2026 Ibiden, a Japanese substrate maker, said it had used only two glass cloth suppliers, in Japan and Taiwan, but now had prospects of a third and fourth for mass production[14]. Because a Japanese supplier, which Ibiden did not name, also plans to add capacity, Ibiden said it expected its material procurement risks to decrease[14].
   - Nitto Boseki says Chinese and Taiwanese companies are aggressively catching up with it in the special glass sector[4]. In July 2026 Nikkei Asia reported that Chinese chip materials makers were competing with Japanese rivals in the market for chip materials, which the industry association SEMI put at $73.2 billion in 2025[34].
   - Most of the firms that build substrates for others are Asian. The White House's 2021 hundred-day supply chain review listed build-up substrate among the materials that commenters called vulnerable[35]. It named key substrate makers including Ibiden, Nanya, Shinko, Samsung, Unimicron, Shennan Circuits, Zhuhai Yueya and AKM, none of them American[10].
   - A May 2024 report by the Semiconductor Industry Association and Boston Consulting Group found that every major package substrate vendor was headquartered in Japan, Taiwan or mainland China[36]. It counted two in Japan, two in Taiwan, one in mainland China and none in the United States[36]. But TTM Technologies, an American circuit board maker, says it offers package substrates too[9].
   - Ibiden will invest about JPY 500 billion in high-performance package substrates from fiscal 2026 to fiscal 2028, starting with about JPY 220 billion, mainly at its Gama plant in Gifu Prefecture[37]. In August 2026 it said the Gama plant was scheduled to start operating in stages from the end of fiscal 2027 and to add to its results from fiscal 2028[14]. In May 2026 management said Ibiden typically takes close to 100 percent of each new generation of what it calls interposer-type substrates at launch[5]. It added that this share may decline by 20 to 30 percent after about three to six months[5]. On silicon-bridge substrates, it said wiring the underside is hard enough that Ibiden expects to keep its technical lead at least through fiscal 2030[5].
   - Unimicron reported NT$42.9 billion of sales in the second quarter of 2026[6]. By technology, 52 percent came from build-up substrates, and by application, 61 percent came from AI data center work[6].
   - Samsung Electro-Mechanics grew package solution sales 37 percent year on year to KRW 771.6 billion in the second quarter of 2026, on substrates for AI accelerators, server processors and cars[7].
   - AT&S, an Austrian firm, calls itself a globally leading maker of high-end package substrates[38]. It makes them in China and Malaysia, and since its 2025/26 financial year also in Austria[39]. Citing the research firm Prismark, its annual report for 2025/26 valued the market for flip-chip ball grid array (FC-BGA) substrates at $7.5 billion in 2025, up 18 percent from 2024[2].
   - Source Unimicron 2026 Q2 earnings conference[6]
   - TTM Technologies says it regularly builds printed circuit boards of more than 30 layers and can go past 70[9]. Those layers carry power to the GPUs and route fast signals between them, so how much signal the laminate absorbs is a main design constraint. Nitto Boseki also makes NE-glass, a low-dielectric glass cloth that absorbs less signal, used in switches and routers in data centers[45].
   - Data center computing was 24 percent of TTM's fiscal 2025 revenue of about $2.9 billion, against 14 percent two years earlier[9].
   - Board makers are adding plants outside China and Taiwan, where TTM says most of its circuit board competitors are based[9]. In its annual report for 2025, TTM said it was building a plant in Syracuse, New York, to make ultra-high-density circuit boards for national security needs[9]. It expected volume production there in the second half of 2026[9]. Nikkei Asia reported in September 2025 that Thailand aimed to become the biggest circuit board hub outside China as board makers moved to diversify production[46].
   - The few firms that build substrates are spending to add capacity, but it takes years. In August 2026 Ibiden said that, with supply short across the industry, the average price of package substrates had risen more than it expected[14]. The materials inside the substrate are harder to replace than the firms that build it. Mitsubishi Gas Chemical, a laminate maker, says chipmakers and electronic device makers further down the chain approve the materials it sells to substrate makers[1]. So a rival to Ajinomoto's film or to Nitto Boseki's low-expansion glass cloth would first need approval from those chipmakers and device makers.

In July 2026 Nikkei Asia reported that Chinese chip materials makers were competing with Japanese rivals in the market for chip materials, which the industry association SEMI put at $73.2 billion in 2025[34].
   + Most of the firms that build substrates for others are Asian. The White House's 2021 hundred-day supply chain review listed build-up substrate among the materials that commenters called vulnerable[35]. It named key substrate makers including Ibiden, Nanya, Shinko, Samsung, Unimicron, Shennan Circuits, Zhuhai Yueya and AKM, none of them American[13].
   + A May 2024 report by the Semiconductor Industry Association and Boston Consulting Group found that every major package substrate vendor was headquartered in Japan, Taiwan or mainland China[36]. It counted two in Japan, two in Taiwan, one in mainland China and none in the United States[36]. But TTM Technologies, an American circuit board maker, says it offers package substrates too[5].
   + Ibiden will invest about JPY 500 billion in high-performance package substrates from fiscal 2026 to fiscal 2028, starting with about JPY 220 billion, mainly at its Gama plant in Gifu Prefecture[37]. In August 2026 it said the Gama plant was scheduled to start operating in stages from the end of fiscal 2027 and to add to its results from fiscal 2028[4]. In May 2026 management said Ibiden typically takes close to 100 percent of each new generation of what it calls interposer-type substrates at launch[9]. It added that this share may decline by 20 to 30 percent after about three to six months[9]. On silicon-bridge substrates, it said wiring the underside is hard enough that Ibiden expects to keep its technical lead at least through fiscal 2030[9].
   + Unimicron reported NT$42.9 billion of sales in the second quarter of 2026[10]. By technology, 52 percent came from build-up substrates, and by application, 61 percent came from AI data center work[10].
   + Samsung Electro-Mechanics grew package solution sales 37 percent year on year to KRW 771.6 billion in the second quarter of 2026, on substrates for AI accelerators, server processors and cars[11].
   + AT&S, an Austrian firm, calls itself a globally leading maker of high-end package substrates[38]. It makes them in China and Malaysia, and since its 2025/26 financial year also in Austria[39]. Citing the research firm Prismark, its annual report for 2025/26 valued the market for flip-chip ball grid array (FC-BGA) substrates at $7.5 billion in 2025, up 18 percent from 2024[7].
   + Source Unimicron 2026 Q2 earnings conference[10]
   + TTM Technologies says it regularly builds printed circuit boards of more than 30 layers and can go past 70[5]. Those layers carry power to the GPUs and route fast signals between them, so how much signal the laminate absorbs is a main design constraint. Nitto Boseki also makes NE-glass, a low-dielectric glass cloth that absorbs less signal, used in switches and routers in data centers[45].
   + Data center computing was 24 percent of TTM's fiscal 2025 revenue of about $2.9 billion, against 14 percent two years earlier[5].
   + Board makers are adding plants outside China and Taiwan, where TTM says most of its circuit board competitors are based[5]. In its annual report for 2025, TTM said it was building a plant in Syracuse, New York, to make ultra-high-density circuit boards for national security needs[5]. It expected volume production there in the second half of 2026[5]. Nikkei Asia reported in September 2025 that Thailand aimed to become the biggest circuit board hub outside China as board makers moved to diversify production[46].
   + The few firms that build substrates are spending to add capacity, but it takes years. In August 2026 Ibiden said that, with supply short across the industry, the average price of package substrates had risen more than it expected[4]. The materials inside the substrate are harder to replace than the firms that build it. Mitsubishi Gas Chemical, a laminate maker, says chipmakers and electronic device makers further down the chain approve the materials it sells to substrate makers[6]. So a rival to Ajinomoto's film or to Nitto Boseki's low-expansion glass cloth would first need approval from those chipmakers and device makers.
== /test-and-assembly/: 313 lines before, 316 after; as printed only before 27, only after 30; marks out only before 1, only after 4; card 49 lines, the same words; order with the card set aside the same
   sources: 78 lines before, 78 after, the same set, in the new reading order
   the card before: after "See it in 3D in the interactive chapter", before "In plain terms"
   the card after:  after "Turning silicon into a finished, packaged chip takes 1,000 to 1,500 st", before "How it works"
   words only before: High
   words only after:  High concentration
   words only after:  ·
   words only after:  Moderately hard to substitute
   words only after:  Chokepoint card
   - High
   - Rated on test tools, where Advantest held 62.7 percent of the market in 2025 and Teradyne 21.8 percent, by CSET's analysis of TechInsights data[1].
   - market for chip testers in 2025, by Advantest's estimate[2]
   - About 65% of the tester market in 2025, by its own estimate[2]
   - $2.52bn of semiconductor test revenue in 2025, service included[3]
   - Ran 8,348 testers in Q2 2026, up from 6,797 a year earlier[4]
   - $6.71bn of 2025 revenue[5]
   - 87% of the $1.9bn market for dicing saws and grinders in 2025, on CSET's analysis of TechInsights data[6]
   - Customers such as ASE already buy testers from both firms[7]. The barrier is cost, because switching means rebuilding a customer's whole test setup, Advantest says[8].
   - Georgetown's CSET, analyzing TechInsights data, finds that between 2019 and 2024 Chinese toolmakers gained 5 percentage points of world market share in test tools and 4 points in fabrication tools built for advanced packaging[9]. But they lost share in bonding and other conventional assembly and packaging tools[9].
   - American firms held 23.5 percent of the market for test tools in 2025 and Japanese firms 63.0 percent, by CSET's analysis of TechInsights data[1]. Amkor raised its planned investment in an Arizona packaging and test campus to $7 billion in October 2025[10]. In June 2026 it announced a long-term partnership with TSMC, which will buy packaging and test services from it in Arizona[11].
   - In a TrendForce ranking cited by CSET, four of the ten largest chip assembly and test contractors by 2024 revenue are headquartered in China[9]. Georgetown's CSET, analyzing TechInsights data, finds that between 2019 and 2024 Chinese toolmakers gained 5 percentage points of world market share in test tools[9]. Across assembly, test and packaging tools, they still lag significantly behind foreign suppliers in market share[9]. Advantest says that switching to another tester maker means rebuilding a customer's whole test setup, at significant extra cost[8].
   - Turning silicon into a finished, packaged chip takes 1,000 to 1,500 steps, depending on how complex the chip is, the Semiconductor Industry Association estimated in 2025[12]. Of all those steps, only test puts electrical signals through the chip, Advantest's investor guide of July 2026 says[13]. The earlier stages of chipmaking make dies, the individual chips on a wafer. The back end of the industry, assembly and test, checks which of them work and puts the good ones into packages.
   - Source Advantest FY2025 results briefing, April 2026[2]
   - Source Advantest FY2025 results briefing, April 2026[2]
   - Advantest puts its own share at about 65 percent of an estimated $9.0 billion tester market in 2025[2]. Its share of system-on-chip (SoC) testers, the machines that test processors and other non-memory chips, rose about ten points in a year to 66 percent[2]. It held about 60 percent of memory test[2].
   - Teradyne, Advantest's main rival, took $2.52 billion of semiconductor test revenue in 2025, service included[3]. Of that, $1.89 billion came from SoC testers and $505 million from memory testers[3]. Teradyne's total revenue grew 13 percent to $3.19 billion in 2025, with fourth-quarter revenue up 44 percent on AI demand[19].
   - Georgetown University's Center for Security and Emerging Technology (CSET) makes a second estimate, from data by the research firm TechInsights[1]. It gives Advantest 62.7 percent of a $9.1 billion market for test tools in 2025 and Teradyne 21.8 percent[1]. Japanese firms held 63.0 percent of that market and American firms 23.5 percent[1].
   - Dicing and grinding. Dicing saws cut wafers into dies, and grinders thin them. CSET's analysis of TechInsights data found that in 2025 Disco held 87 percent and Accretech 8.1 percent of the $1.9 billion market for these machines[6]. Panel-level packaging builds packages on large panels in place of 300 mm wafers, to make more of them at once[25]. In December 2025 Disco announced a saw that cuts such panels, as large as 400 by 400 mm, into individual packages[25].
   - Taiwanese firms lead outsourced assembly and test, the OSAT business, and Chinese firms are large challengers. Taiwan's ASE billed $20,573.4 million in 2025[7]. Packaging made up about 48 percent of that, testing about 11 percent and its electronics manufacturing arm about 40 percent[31]. In a TrendForce ranking that CSET cites, four of the top ten OSATs by 2024 revenue are headquartered in China[9].
   - Taiwan's assembly and test contractors are expanding. ASE ran 6,797 testers in the second quarter of 2025 and 8,348 a year later[4]. It spent $2.7 billion on machinery and $1.4 billion on buildings, facilities and automation in the first half of 2026[35]. In July 2026 it said revenue from leading-edge advanced packaging was running ahead of its $3.5 billion forecast for 2026[35]. Powertech, another Taiwanese assembly and test firm, aims to run the first panel-level packaging line for AI chips in 2027[36].
   - Amkor, an American assembly and test contractor, took $6.71 billion of revenue in 2025[5]. Its revenue in the second quarter of 2026 alone was $1.90 billion, up 26 percent year on year[37]. By October 2025 it had raised the planned investment in its Arizona campus to $7 billion[10].
   - In October 2024 TSMC and Amkor signed a memorandum of understanding for TSMC to buy packaging and test services from Amkor's planned plant in Peoria, Arizona[38]. TSMC would use those services for its customers, especially those using its Phoenix fabs[38]. The two announced a long-term partnership in June 2026[11].
   - Source ASE Technology Holding, second quarter 2026 earnings release (SEC Form 6-K)[4]
   - Customers can switch tester makers, but at a cost. Advantest says that switching to another tester maker means rebuilding the whole test setup used for development, evaluation and mass production, at significant extra cost to the customer[8]. Still, ASE says it buys testers with similar functions from different makers, including Teradyne and Advantest[7].
   - Chinese toolmakers are gaining share in test and advanced packaging tools, but CSET finds they still lag significantly behind foreign suppliers in market share[9]. Between 2019 and 2024 they gained 5 percentage points of world market share in test tools and 4 points in fabrication tools built for advanced packaging, by CSET's analysis of TechInsights data[9]. But they lost share in bonding and other conventional assembly and packaging tools[9].
   - Assembly and test contractors such as ASE and Amkor earn a small part of what the chip industry sells. ASE's packaging business took $9,829.2 million from outside customers in 2025, and its testing business $2,292.0 million[7]. Amkor's revenue was $6.71 billion[5]. Global semiconductor sales that year were $791.7 billion[39].

== /systems-and-networking/: 308 lines before, 311 after; as printed only before 24, only after 27; marks out only before 1, only after 4; card 49 lines, the same words; order with the card set aside the same
   sources: 88 lines before, 88 after, the same set, in the new reading order
   the card before: after "See it in 3D in the interactive chapter", before "In plain terms"
   the card after:  after "Before a chip package can compute, it has to be built into a machine. ", before "How it works"
   words only before: High
   words only after:  High concentration
   words only after:  ·
   words only after:  Moderately hard to substitute
   words only after:  Chokepoint card
   - High
   - Rated on NVLink and its NVSwitch chips, which Nvidia controls and which join the accelerators in a rack[1]. Separately, lasers for the optical links were scarce in 2026[2]. Nvidia secured future capacity rights for them from Lumentum in March 2026[3].
   - for one GB200 NVL72 rack bought by a typical hyperscaler, or about $3.9 million with networking, storage and other items, by SemiAnalysis's August 2025 estimate[4]
   - NVLink, InfiniBand and Spectrum-X Ethernet, which it credited for data center growth in the quarter to April 2026[5]
   - $16.7B of AI semiconductor revenue in the quarter to August 2026, up 221%[6]
   - World's largest AI server provider, its chairman said in March 2026[7]
   - High-speed optical modules made in Suzhou and, since 2019, Thailand[8]
   - Lasers and optical networking products, with a $2B Nvidia investment agreed in March 2026[9]
   - UALink is an open specification for the same job as NVLink[10]. AMD's Helios rack, launched in July 2026, runs it over Ethernet[11]. Since May 2025 Nvidia's NVLink Fusion program has let other firms' custom chips use NVLink, which keeps those firms tied to Nvidia's interconnect[12].
   - Chinese firms such as InnoLight assemble high-speed optical modules in volume, at sites including Suzhou and, since 2019, Thailand[8]. In February 2025 China put indium under new export restrictions[13]. The same month it began requiring export permits for indium phosphide substrates, the wafers that lasers for optical links are grown on[14].
   - In March 2026 Nvidia agreed to invest $2 billion in the laser maker Lumentum, alongside a multibillion-dollar purchase commitment and rights to future capacity for advanced laser components[3]. Indium phosphide is used in high-speed lasers for optical communications, and China produced 70 percent of the world's indium in 2025[13].
   - On 4 February 2025 China added indium phosphide substrates, the wafers the lasers are grown on, to its export control list[14]. AXT, a US-listed maker of those wafers, does all its manufacturing in China[14]. In its filing for the quarter to June 2026, AXT said it could not estimate when it would resume shipping the wafers to the United States[14].
   - Rack-scale systems drop the baseboard and put the chips on trays stacked in the rack. An Nvidia GB300 NVL72 holds 18 compute trays plus nine switch trays, 72 GPUs in all, and draws up to 142 kW[17]. The newer Vera Rubin NVL72 uses the same rack footprint[18]. The research firm SemiAnalysis estimated in August 2025 that a rack of the earlier GB200 NVL72 model cost a typical hyperscaler $3.1 million, or about $3.9 million with networking, storage and other items[4].
   - UALink. The open specification, from the UALink Consortium, runs 200 Gb/s per lane, and four lanes, which it calls a station, carry 800 Gb/s[10]. It can address up to 1,024 endpoints, such as accelerators, in one group[10]. AMD's Helios rack, launched in July 2026, joins 72 GPUs and runs UALink over Ethernet for its scale-up network[11].
   - Nvidia sells switches and network cards for both InfiniBand and Ethernet. Its data center revenue, which counts its GPUs as well as its networking gear, reached $89.0 billion in the quarter to July 2026, up 117 percent from a year earlier[29]. In the quarter before, it credited its data center growth to Blackwell 300 products and to demand for its "InfiniBand, Spectrum-X Ethernet, and NVLink" solutions[5].
   - Chinese makers of optical modules have built plants outside China. InnoLight has run a plant in Thailand since 2019, alongside its sites in Suzhou and Taiwan[8]. The Thai subsidiary of Eoptolink, another Chinese module maker, lists sites in Chonburi and Rayong[33].
   - China produces most of the world's indium, the raw material for indium phosphide lasers, and restricts its export. Indium phosphide is used in the high-speed laser diodes and photodetectors for optical communications[13]. China accounted for 70 percent of world indium production in 2025[13]. In February 2025 China's Ministry of Commerce placed indium under new export restrictions[13].
   - AI data centers were straining supplies of the lasers inside optical modules and pushing up their prices, Nikkei Asia reported in May 2026[2]. Nvidia had agreed in March 2026 to invest $2 billion in one laser maker, Lumentum, alongside a multibillion-dollar purchase commitment and rights to future capacity for advanced laser components[3]. Coherent, another laser maker, broke ground in Sherman, Texas, in June 2026 on an expansion of its factory there, which makes optical devices from indium phosphide[15].
   - Two American firms, Broadcom and Marvell, sell both custom AI chips and Ethernet switch chips. In its annual report for fiscal 2025, Broadcom said its AI chips include Ethernet switch chips as well as custom accelerators, which it builds to each customer's specifications[39]. Its AI semiconductor revenue reached $16.7 billion in the third quarter of fiscal 2026, up 221 percent from a year earlier[6]. Marvell also builds custom AI chips to each customer's specifications and sells Ethernet switch chips, its annual report for fiscal 2026 says[40]. Marvell's five-year agreement with Amazon Web Services, announced in December 2024, covers custom AI chips alongside the optical and networking parts that go with them[41].
   - Taiwanese firms do much of the physical assembly. Foxconn booked NT$8.1 trillion of revenue in 2025, up 18 percent, and cloud and networking was its largest product category in the fourth quarter[7]. In the same results release, its chairman called Foxconn the world's largest AI server provider[7].
   - Indium phosphide laser capacity. Nvidia has secured rights to future capacity from laser makers. Alongside its Lumentum deal, in March 2026 Nvidia agreed to invest $2 billion in Coherent[9]. The deal also includes a multibillion-dollar purchase commitment and rights to future access and capacity for Coherent's advanced laser and optical networking products[9].
   - Indium phosphide substrates. China added indium phosphide substrates, the wafers the lasers are grown on, to its export control list on 4 February 2025[14]. AXT, a US-listed maker of these wafers, does all its manufacturing in China[14]. In its filing for the quarter to June 2026, AXT called the need for Chinese export permits the most significant challenge it faces[14]. It said it could not estimate when it would resume shipping the wafers to the United States[14]. The same filing names Sumitomo and JX, both Japanese firms, as AXT's competitors in indium phosphide[14].
   - NVSwitch. Nvidia's annual report for fiscal 2026 lists NVLink interconnects and switches among its own networking products[1]. UALink is an open standard designed for accelerator communications within one scale-up group[10]. Since May 2025 Nvidia's NVLink Fusion program has let other firms' custom chips use NVLink, including CPUs that Fujitsu and Qualcomm plan to connect to Nvidia GPUs[12].
   - Optical module assembly can move, as InnoLight's high-volume plant in Thailand, opened in 2019, shows[8].

Marvell's five-year agreement with Amazon Web Services, announced in December 2024, covers custom AI chips alongside the optical and networking parts that go with them[41].
   + Taiwanese firms do much of the physical assembly. Foxconn booked NT$8.1 trillion of revenue in 2025, up 18 percent, and cloud and networking was its largest product category in the fourth quarter[9]. In the same results release, its chairman called Foxconn the world's largest AI server provider[9].
   + Indium phosphide laser capacity. Nvidia has secured rights to future capacity from laser makers. Alongside its Lumentum deal, in March 2026 Nvidia agreed to invest $2 billion in Coherent[11]. The deal also includes a multibillion-dollar purchase commitment and rights to future access and capacity for Coherent's advanced laser and optical networking products[11].
   + Indium phosphide substrates. China added indium phosphide substrates, the wafers the lasers are grown on, to its export control list on 4 February 2025[3]. AXT, a US-listed maker of these wafers, does all its manufacturing in China[3]. In its filing for the quarter to June 2026, AXT called the need for Chinese export permits the most significant challenge it faces[3]. It said it could not estimate when it would resume shipping the wafers to the United States[3]. The same filing names Sumitomo and JX, both Japanese firms, as AXT's competitors in indium phosphide[3].
   + NVSwitch. Nvidia's annual report for fiscal 2026 lists NVLink interconnects and switches among its own networking products[4]. UALink is an open standard designed for accelerator communications within one scale-up group[12]. Since May 2025 Nvidia's NVLink Fusion program has let other firms' custom chips use NVLink, including CPUs that Fujitsu and Qualcomm plan to connect to Nvidia GPUs[14].
   + Optical module assembly can move, as InnoLight's high-volume plant in Thailand, opened in 2019, shows[10].
== /data-centers-and-power/: 329 lines before, 332 after; as printed only before 39, only after 42; marks out only before 1, only after 4; card 49 lines, the same words; order with the card set aside the same
   sources: 72 lines before, 72 after, the same set, in the new reading order
   the card before: after "See it in 3D in the interactive chapter", before "In plain terms"
   the card after:  after "A wafer passes through a leading-edge fab in months, but in 2024 a lar", before "How it works"
   words only before: High
   words only after:  High concentration
   words only after:  ·
   words only after:  Moderately hard to substitute
   words only after:  Chokepoint card
   - High
   - Nvidia named nine data center power system providers in October 2025 for its 800-volt designs for gigawatt-scale data centers[1].
   - of data center investment expected worldwide in 2025, in the International Energy Agency's November 2025 outlook[2]
   - Gas equipment, transformers and switchgear, including through Prolec GE[3]
   - Power transformers and switchgear[3]
   - Grid technologies, transformers, turbines[3]
   - Power generation equipment, including gas turbines[3]
   - Data center power systems[1]
   - Cooling, through its Motivair division[4]
   - Buyers of grid equipment can choose among suppliers, and GE Vernova's 2025 annual report names Hitachi Energy, Siemens Energy, Schneider Electric and ABB among its key competitors[3]. But in the United States in 2024 the longest large power transformer lead times reached 60 months[5]. An International Energy Agency survey published in 2025 found waits of up to four years worldwide[6].
   - China used 25 percent of the world's data center electricity in 2024, second to the United States, by the International Energy Agency's estimate[7]. Data centers made up around 3 percent of the growth in China's electricity demand from 2022 to 2024[8].
   - US factories supplied 29.2 percent of the large power transformers bought in the United States in 2023, measured by capacity[9]. Constellation said in August 2026 that it expects to restart the Crane nuclear plant in 2027[10]. Microsoft has agreed to buy the plant's power[11].
   - A data center feeds electricity to the machines inside it and carries away the heat they give off. One rack of AI chips, a cabinet taller than a door, can draw 142 kilowatts[12]. Every watt of it comes back out as heat, and liquid piped through the rack carries the heat away[12]. A large training site already holds more than a hundred thousand chips[13]. The newest sites are planned in gigawatts[14]. One gigawatt is the whole output of a large power station, and Epoch AI projects that OpenAI's seven Stargate sites in the United States will add up to more than nine of them by 2029[14]. Getting that much electricity to the site is harder than putting up the building. Transformers and heavy switches connect a site to the grid, and turbines make the power. All of them come from a few suppliers and take years to arrive.
   - Measured by capacity, US factories supplied 29.2 percent of the large power transformers bought in the US in 2023, and imports the rest[9]. GE Vernova's gas equipment backlog and reserved factory slots stood at 116 GW in mid-2026, against planned output of 20 GW a year from the third quarter[15].
   - ERCOT, the grid operator for most of Texas, counted 9,456 MW of big power users approved to energize in its overview for September 2026[16]. Judging by peak monthly use in September, it believed 4,132 MW of that was operating[16]. On 3 August 2026 Governor Greg Abbott ordered an audit of every data center seeking a connection before any data center project moves forward[17]. In September 2026 ERCOT said it had paused approvals to energize data centers of 75 MW or more until the audit and a review of their impact on local communities are complete[18].
   - A wafer passes through a leading-edge fab in months, but in 2024 a large power transformer commonly took three years to arrive[5]. Before the pandemic, the same order took under a year[19].
   - The rack is the basic unit of AI compute. Nvidia's GB300 NVL72 rack draws up to 142 kW through eight 33 kW power shelves, the rack's own power supplies[12]. Large sites hold many such racks. Colossus 1, an AI data center in Memphis, Tennessee, held an estimated 230,000 Nvidia GPUs by July 2025[13].
   - Heat leaves in water. Cold plates, metal blocks with coolant running through them, sit on the GPU packages and pass the heat to the building's water loop, which releases it outdoors[4]. Nvidia's Rubin racks run coolant at up to 45 °C, warm enough that in many climates the loop can release its heat without turning on chillers, the refrigeration machines that cool water[4].
   - Air. Heat exchangers in the rack door work when density, the power each rack draws, is moderate, but Nvidia's GB300 NVL72 rack is liquid cooled[12].
   - Direct-to-chip liquid. Nvidia's Rubin platform is 100 percent liquid cooled, and Nvidia says every cloud provider and data center operator building for it is moving to liquid[4].
   - Nvidia says the cooling supply chain is keeping pace with the move to liquid[4]. Motivair, Schneider Electric's cooling division, has worked with Nvidia for nearly a decade, following Nvidia's product roadmap[4].
   - Nvidia names nine data center power system providers for its 800-volt designs for gigawatt-scale data centers, among them ABB, Eaton, Hitachi Energy, Mitsubishi Electric, Schneider Electric, Siemens and Vertiv[1].
   - A megawatt rack changes the busbar, the floor loading, the coolant flow, the leak detection and the energy stored in the rack. Nvidia's Vera Rubin NVL72 rack design adds energy storage to keep power steady[1].
   - Grid interconnection. The slow path in the United States, where the queues to connect are long[14].
   - Behind-the-meter gas, generation on the site itself. The fast path, which at least three of OpenAI's seven US Stargate sites will use to avoid grid queues[14].
   - Nuclear. Existing plants already sell power to data centers[22]. Talen Energy supplies Amazon's data center campus next to the Susquehanna nuclear plant in Pennsylvania under a June 2025 contract expected to reach 1,920 MW no later than 2032[22]. Restarts and new reactors are still to come. Constellation signed a power purchase agreement with Microsoft to restart Three Mile Island Unit 1 as the 835 MW Crane Clean Energy Center[11]. The plant shut in 2019[23]. A $1 billion federal loan that closed in November 2025 partly finances its restart, which is subject to licensing approvals from the US Nuclear Regulatory Commission[23].
   - The largest data center operators pay for the buildout. Capital spending by Alphabet, Amazon, Meta, Microsoft and Oracle came to $448.2 billion in 2025, the sum of quarterly figures compiled by Epoch AI, a research group[24]. If the trend continues, it will reach $770 billion in 2026[24]. The International Energy Agency expected worldwide investment in data centers to reach $580 billion in 2025[2].
   - Epoch AI also projects that OpenAI's seven US Stargate sites will exceed 9 GW by 2029[14]. It expects the site in Abilene, Texas, to grow from an estimated 0.3 GW in April 2026 to 1.2 GW in the fourth quarter of 2026[14].
   - A few large firms make the turbines, transformers and switchgear. GE Vernova builds large gas turbines, and its 2025 annual report names Siemens Energy and Mitsubishi Power among the key competitors of its power generation business[3]. Its electrification business makes power transformers and switchgear, and the report names Hitachi Energy, Siemens Energy, Schneider Electric and ABB among that business's key competitors[3].
   - Equipment makers limit how fast the buildout can go. GE Vernova's gas equipment backlog and reserved factory slots grew from 100 GW to 116 GW in the second quarter of 2026[15]. In the first half of 2026, data center orders for its electrification business, which makes grid equipment, passed $5 billion, more than double that business's data center orders for all of 2025[15]. GE Vernova bought the remaining half of Prolec GE, a transformer maker, in the first half of 2026[15].
   - Source GE Vernova second-quarter 2026 results, July 2026[15]
   - The US Energy Information Administration expects electricity demand to keep rising, but in September 2026 it cut its 2027 forecast for the region that includes Texas. In its October 2026 Short-Term Energy Outlook, it expects US electricity sales of 4,152.0 TWh in 2026 and 4,216.6 TWh in 2027[27]. It expects electricity demand from the commercial sector, which includes data centers, to grow 2.8 percent in 2027 and demand from industry to grow 2.7 percent[28]. Its September outlook had lowered the 2027 electricity sales forecast for the region that includes Texas by 4.7 percent from August[29]. That outlook noted that Texas had paused connecting new data centers to the grid while the state audits proposed projects[30]. The pause, set by the grid operator for most of Texas, covers data centers and crypto mining sites of 75 MW or more[18].
   - By the International Energy Agency's estimate, China accounted for 25 percent of the world's data center electricity use in 2024, second to the United States at 45 percent[7]. Data centers made up around 3 percent of the growth in China's electricity demand from 2022 to 2024, the agency found[8].
   - On the Texas grid, big power users have asked for far more electricity than is connected and running. ERCOT, the grid operator for most of Texas, was tracking about 474 GW of large loads (big power users) seeking a connection in June 2026, roughly 90 percent of it data centers[32]. In its operational overview for September 2026, ERCOT counted 9,456 MW of large loads approved to energize, that is, cleared to switch on[16]. Judging by peak monthly use in September, it believed only 4,132 MW of it was operating[16].
   - Before a big user can connect, engineers must study whether the nearby grid can carry its load. In a preliminary review in July 2026, ERCOT found that about 205 GW of the requests already had such a study, which made them eligible for Batch Zero, the first batch of requests it studies together[33]. In September 2026 ERCOT conditionally classed 66.4 GW of Batch Zero projects as base load and 127.9 GW as studied load, pending final decisions in December[18]. Under ERCOT's Batch Zero rules, base load is demand from projects already running or far enough along to be counted without further study[34]. Studied load is demand that ERCOT will study before giving each project a share of the grid's capacity[34].
   - Texas has paused new connections for large data centers while it audits them. On 3 August 2026 Texas Governor Greg Abbott ordered the state's Public Utility Commission and ERCOT to audit every data center waiting to connect to the grid before any data center project moves forward[17]. ERCOT then paused approvals to energize data centers and crypto mining sites of 75 MW or more until the audit and a review of their impact on local communities are complete[18]. It will report on the audit and the community review to the Public Utility Commission by 10 December 2026[18].
   - Large power transformers. In the United States in 2024, lead times of 36 months were commonly quoted and the longest reached 60 months, against under a year before the pandemic[5]. By a US Commerce Department estimate, 82 percent of the units put into US service in 2019 were imported[35]. In an industry survey published in 2025, the International Energy Agency found that large power transformers took up to four years to secure worldwide[6]. It also found that their average lead times had almost doubled since 2021[6]. A transformer's size is given as a power rating in megavolt-amperes (MVA)[5]. Measured by capacity, US factories supplied 29.2 percent of the transformers rated 60 MVA and above bought in the US in 2023, and imports supplied the rest[9].
   - Gas turbines. GE Vernova's gas equipment backlog and reserved factory slots stood at 116 GW in mid-2026[15]. In July 2026 it expected to build gas turbines at a yearly rate of 20 GW in the third quarter of 2026, 24 GW in 2028 and 30 GW in 2030[15].
   - Firm generation, the kind that runs whatever the weather. The Nuclear Regulatory Commission has approved a fuel license amendment for the Crane plant[10]. Constellation called the approval a major milestone toward restarting the plant in 2027[10].
